#149. EU and Indonesia sign trade deal

A deal covering 730 million people promises lower tariffs and new investment opportunities as the European Union seeks to diversify trade ties amid growing global economic tensions.

The European Union and Indonesia signed a free trade deal on Saturday, describing it as a new chapter in economic relations between the two sides.

"This is one of the most ambitious deals the European Union has concluded in Asia," Ursula von der Leyen, president of the European Commission, said at the signing ceremony on Saturday.

"Together, we represent a market of 730 million people," she added.

The Indonesia-European Union Comprehensive Economic Partnership Agreement (CEPA) and the additional Investment Protection Agreement (IPA) are designed to reduce trade barriers and attract investment in strategic sectors such as electric vehicles, electronics, and pharmaceuticals.

The agreement comes after almost 10 years of negotiations and is part of a wider series of trade deals by the EU.

The bloc hopes to reduce its reliance on individual economic partners as trade with both China and the US has become more difficult in recent years.

Von der Leyen also stressed the importance of international cooperation, saying that "in a time when others are closing their doors, we keep ours open."

The deal sees tariffs on about 98% of EU exports to Indonesia to be done away with. The EU estimates that European companies could save more than €600 million (around $658 million) per year.

"The benefits for our exporters are concrete and measurable," Indonesian Minister of Economic Affairs Airlangga Hartarto said, hailing a "new chapter" in economic relations with the EU. He said the deal would benefit his country’s main exports to Europe, including palm oil, footwear and textiles.

Palm oil and deforestation had been major stumbling blocks during the negotiations, and the agreement includes mechanisms for cooperation on regulatory and sustainability issues as well as a dedicated protocol on palm oil to address environmental concerns.

The EU parliament is now expected to greenlight the deal while Indonesian authorities will also complete the ratification process so it can enter into force in 2027.

#148. Species in tropical rainforests may be surprisingly young, study finds

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

A study of the tropical fern genus Danaea suggests that while the lineage is ancient, many of its living species arose only within the last 6 million years. Researchers link that recent diversification to major geological and climatic changes in tropical America, including the uplift of the Andes, changes in soil and climate, and the drainage of western Amazon wetlands.

#147. Indian man sentenced to 19 years in prison for conspiring to kill Sikh separatist in New York

Nikhil Gupta admitted to plotting with Indian government official to hire hitman to murder dual US-Canadian citizen

An Indian ⁠man was sentenced to more ⁠than 19 ​years in US prison on Friday after admitting to orchestrating a failed Indian government-backed plot to kill a Sikh separatist in New York ⁠City, in connection with what US and Canadian authorities have called a broader effort to target Indian dissidents.

Nikhil Gupta, 55, pleaded guilty in February to murder-for-hire, conspiracy ⁠to commit murder-for-hire and conspiracy to commit money laundering.

The US district judge Victor Marrero handed down the ​235-month sentence at a hearing in a Manhattan federal ‌court.

The discovery of alleged ‌assassination plots against Sikh separatists in the US and Canada has tested relations with India, which has ‌denied involvement in such plots.

Prosecutors accused Gupta of plotting with an Indian government official to kill Gurpatwant Singh Pannun, a dual US-Canadian citizen and lawyer at Sikhs for Justice in New York who has advocated for a sovereign Sikh state in northern India.

Gupta and the Indian government official, Vikash Yadav, arranged to have $15,000 in cash delivered to a purported hitman who ‌was actually an undercover US law enforcement officer, court papers show.

Yadav is also charged in the case but is not in US custody. It is unclear ​whether he has hired a defense lawyer.

India’s government has dissociated itself from any plot against Pannun, saying it was against government policy.

Prosecutors with the Manhattan US attorney’s office had recommended a sentence of about 24 years in prison.

“This case cuts to the very core of American values,” prosecutor Ashley ⁠Nicolas said in court. “We don’t condone the use of violence to silence voices ​in this country, ever.”

Addressing the ​court on Friday, Gupta said ​he regretted straying from the non-violent example set by the Indian independence leader Mahatma Gandhi.

“I’m ​sorry to Mr ‌Pannun, I’m sorry ​to the communities I hurt, ​I’m sorry to my own family,” Gupta said.

Pannun told the court that he was grateful to the US for prosecuting Gupta and said he would keep advocating for Sikh sovereignty.

“Self-determination for my people and my homeland, Punjab, is my lifetime goal,” he said.

#145. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#144. When you see smoke, run: Rohingya who escaped Myanmar’s genocide tell their stories

Survivors recount rivers red with blood and burning nearby villages serving as an urgent warning to flee their homes

Smoke was often the signal to run – seen from a distance, it was the indication for Rohingya that a neighbouring village was under attack and that theirs would be next.

In other places, they saw the rivers run red and would pull out the bodies, perform quick burial rites and then flee.

What happened during the escape from Myanmar – a journey through forests and over hills and rivers while being battered by monsoon rains – was a key part of the ruling made this week in The Hague that Myanmar had carried out a genocide against the Rohingya.

The international court of justice (ICJ) said it was not enough to say they had faced ethnic cleansing when soldiers had also killed those trying to escape – Myanmar was trying to destroy the group as a people.

Stories of that violence in 2017, of organised mass killing, sexual attacks and the complete destruction of their villages, came with the refugees who arrived in neighbouring Bangladesh, eventually numbering more than 700,000 and settling in what has become the world’s largest refugee camp.

For months, they would arrive from places such as Tula Toli, where thousands were rounded up, killed and buried in mass graves. In other villages, men were captured and killed by the security forces.

Many survivors had escaped an attack on their own village, only to watch their relatives being killed in the villages where they had sought safety.

Almost a decade later, the majority of Rohingya who arrived in 2017 remain in the refugee camps, joining those who had already been there since the early 1990s, having escaped previous military operations and ever-increasing persecution.

The ICJ ruling has come at a particularly harsh time for Rohingya refugees, in a year in which hundreds have died at sea trying to escape the refugee camps, and in which countries such as Malaysia and India have spoken about returning the Rohingya to Myanmar.

Rohingya campaigners are hoping that this ruling will put an end to that.

Rohingya refugees still live in fragile shelters made of bamboo and tarpaulin sheets. They are denied the right to leave the camps, to work or to seek education.

Below are some of the stories from Rohingya who arrived in Bangladesh in 2017, having escaped Tula Toli and the surrounding area.

Sitting in the background, almost unnoticed as Momtaz Begum recollected how Myanmar forces had killed her husband and children and then raped her in front of her lone surviving child, was a woman who seemed to know every word of the story.

It was not because she had heard it before. Dildar Begum had experienced exactly the same violence as her sister had, in their village, Tula Toli.

In fact, they had for months believed each other dead until they discovered that they had both made it to Bangladesh and lived in different parts of the camp.

Each had only one surviving daughter, both of whom had similar scars along the back of their heads.

Twice previously Anwara Begum had lived as a refugee in Bangladesh. Once as a child in 1978 and then again in the early 1990s, as a young mother who lost her son to a respiratory disease during a rainy season spent in a flimsy tent.

When she was forced to return to Myanmar, though the persecution she fled there had not stopped, Anwara swore to herself that she would never become a refugee again.

She broke that oath when she saw the river by her village filled with bodies floating from Tula Toli.

Her husband first pulled from the river as many bodies as he could to bury and then they gathered some belongings and headed westwards to the River Naf – the border with Bangladesh.

To cross, she handed over all the gold she owned, equal to US $200 (£150), to a Bangladeshi boatman.

Elderly people were not spared the Myanmar military’s attacks. Like so many others, Nur Alam had to be carried on the shoulders of his children on his journey to Bangladesh.

His 10-year-old grandson, Mamoon, held his hand. Once in the camp, they stuck to each other.

Alam’s arrival in Bangladesh was his fourth time being a refugee there. He was first expelled from Myanmar – known as Burma until 1989 – during military operations in the 1950s when Bangladesh was not yet an independent country.

#142. Russian helicopter suspected of violating Finnish airspace

Finland's Defense Ministry thinks the incursion is linked to a drone strike earlier Saturday on a Russian cargo ship, which is on a US sanctions list.

A Russian helicopter is suspected of violating Finnish airspace, the Nordic country's Ministry of Defense said Saturday.

The incident happened a few hours after a drone attack left a Russian cargo ship ablaze in the Gulf of Finland, the easternmost part of the Baltic Sea.

According to Finland's Defense Ministry, the incursion happened at 12:34 p.m. local time (10:34 UTC/GMT) off the town of Loviisa, on the southern coast.

"This alleged airspace violation may be linked to rescue or surveillance operations carried out by Finland following damage sustained by a cargo ship in the Gulf of Finland," public broadcaster YLE cited Defense Minister Antti Hakkanen as saying.

The ministry added the helicopter could have penetrated up to 4.1 kilometers (2.5 miles) into Finnish territory.

Earlier Saturday, Finland — one of NATO's newest members — said a Russian cargo ship heading for Russia's Baltic exclave of Kaliningrad caught fire after being struck by drones in the Gulf of Finland.

The Finnish military said the Sparta II was damaged ⁠west ⁠of Russia's Gogland island, about 40 kilometers (25 miles) from Finland's coast, and later drifted into Finnish territorial waters.

Finnish ‌Border ​Guard Commander Ilja Iljin later told YLE that the vessel, which is known to have links to the Russian military, was struck by Ukrainian  drones while in Russian waters.

"At the moment, ​there is no confirmed information ⁠about ​what cargo ​is on board, ​but it ‌cannot be ruled out ​that ⁠there is military materiel on ⁠board," ​Iljin said.

The ship is on a US sanctions list, according to a search of the Open Sanctions website.

The Border Guard later said the crew of the Sparta II was evacuating into a life raft and rescue boat.

"The [Ukraine] war is spilling over into our neighbouring areas," wrote Finnish Prime Minister Petteri Orpo on X.

Ukraine has the right to fight against the aggressor," he added.

The Finnish government has put temporary curbs on aviation and maritime traffic in the eastern Gulf of Finland.

The Defense Ministry said Finland had informed its allies about the incident.

#141. Israel delays building contracts for West Bank settlement after UK sanctions

Attorney general says E1 settlement decision should not be taken by caretaker government led by Netanyahu

Israel has postponed issuing contracts for the construction of a large settlement on the West Bank seen as likely to render a two-state solution unviable, days after the UK imposed sanctions against building firms operating in the area.

Israeli diplomats insisted the move was not in response to the measures from Downing Street. Gali Baharav-Miara, the attorney general, said any decision on settlement expansion should not be taken by the caretaker government led by Benjamin Netanyahu but by whatever new administration was formed.

She ordered the Israel land authority and ministry of housing not to open the tender process. Israeli diplomats suggested the UK foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, had acted prematurely on Monday in sanctioning the eight construction firms. But UK diplomats believe the growing political pressure on Israel over settlements, including from US Democrats, is having an impact.

The deferral puts the E1 settlement expansion in the mix for any coalition negotiations. The leader of the Change bloc, Gadi Eisenkot, seen as Israel’s most likely next prime minister, has been clear in opposing violent settler activity, as has the newly elected Knesset speaker, Matan Kahana, an ally of Eisenkot.

Eisenkot has also opposed the E1 settlement expansion, describing it as a mistake likely to alienate international opinion. He said of the plan: “I recall that Netanyahu himself opposed this for a long time, and due to coalition pressure, he gave in and approved it.”

The EI settlement expansion, due to have been announced on Thursday, has long been seen as qualitatively different since the land – between Jerusalem and Ma’aleh Adumim, a West Bank settlement city of nearly 40,000 residents – bisects the West Bank and cuts it off from East Jerusalem.

Miliband has said it would mark the death knell of a future Palestinian state. The UK, along with a group of European countries, has recently recognised Palestine, and the preservation of a two-state solution has been described as the north star of Labour policy towards the region.

The construction tender was published for more than 3,401 housing units in the area. At the beginning of November, the Israel Defense Forces began demolishing homes in a Bedouin community within the E1 area.

Israel has already enforced the downgrading of the UK consulate in East Jerusalem as a reprisal for Miliband’s decision in September to try to end trade links with the illegal settlements.

Bennett said: “The British Labour government’s hostility, bias and one-sided approach are particularly glaring given its failure to take any action against the Palestinian Authority, which continues to encourage terrorism through financial payments, promote incitement in its education system and engage in illegal construction in violation of signed agreements.”

Although the Israeli election was largely fought on domestic security-related issues, Eisenkot seems to be more sensitive to the implications of Israel’s crumbling global reputation, and the need to make efforts to be seen to be listening to the criticism.

If he becomes prime minister, Eisenkot will, indirectly at least, also have to take account of the views of the Arab parties even if these parties are not going to become members of the government.

The group Peace Now welcomed the attorney general’s decision: “A government that lost the elections is not permitted to exploit its final days in power to advance destructive moves that will endanger Israel’s future and its international standing.

Construction in E1 is liable to eliminate the possibility of a two-state solution and drag Israel into a severe diplomatic crisis and international sanctions. The AG’s decision is an important step, but the struggle is not over yet.”

#140. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#137. These dead pets were meant to be cremated - then their bodies appeared on a livestream

Revecca Calling is devastated after hearing about the bodies that were found and wants to know if her dog, Olaf, really was cremated

A man â bald, goateed, wearing red-tinted sunglasses â walks down a cracked back road, guiding the camera towards a cluster of plastic-wrapped objects that spill out of the tall grass.

Names are written on the packages â Brandon; Ira; Lucky; Sweet â and numbers. One says 29kg (4.5 stone). Another says 2.5kg. Through the plastic you can make out what appears to be fur, claws and a flattened ear.

It's a mass dumping, the man explains. "We have about 25 animals here that have been dumped in bags. A couple of cats, some dogs."

Warning: This story contains images some might find upsetting

In the weeks since that video was streamed on Facebook, Chris and Geraldine Taylor, who run animal rescue NGO Luna and Furry Siblings (LAFS), say they have uncovered 15 dumping sites in the suburbs of Bulacan, near the Philippines' capital of Manila.

In total they've unearthed the remains of some 170 dead pets. Belgian Malinois, golden retrievers, pugs, puppies and cats have been found dumped off service roads, sealed in septic tanks, floating in rivers and buried in vacant lots.

Many of these animals are thought to have been sent to cremation businesses in the expectation they would be turned to ash.

Now, many grieving owners are having to confront a distressing reality: their beloved pets may not have received the send-off they paid for. The urn in their home may not contain their pet at all. And they may in fact see their so-called fur child again, jettisoned like common waste on the side of a highway.

One saw the livestream and wrote on Facebook: "I think one of those pets is mine." Below, they attached a photo of a beaming Alsatian.

A string of grisly discoveries in the suburbs near Manila has put thousands of grieving pet owners on edge

When Chris arrived on that back road three weeks ago, he couldn't have known he was uncorking a scandal that would see at least one business reportedly shut, four people placed under investigation, and a personal invite to sit down with a senator to discuss animal-cruelty laws.

Chris, a former police officer, and his wife are retired, but only formally. For the past three years, they've been running an animal rescue shelter out of their home in San Jose del Monte, 30km (18.6 miles) north-east of central Manila.

On 30 October, he'd driven into town to report a case of animal neglect at the local district office. When he arrived, he was told the official in charge had just left to respond to another complaint: a report of dumped animals on a nearby roadside.

Chris had seen this once before: 14 dogs that were found and soon reburied. This time, he followed the official to the scene and started documenting â taking photos, scanning the bodies for microchips and uploading footage to his Facebook page.

"I figured there's got to be more to this," he explains. "So we put out word: if anybody knows anything, any other dump locations... just report it. Let us know."

In the days that followed, tip-offs started trickling in. More dumpsites emerged.

"The long vacant lot on the left side... that's where the smell comes from," read one message.

"When we ran to this place it smelled of rotting animals," read another, accompanied by a photo of a map with a location circled in red.

Chris Taylor, pictured here with a dog unrelated to the scandal, has uncovered the remains of some 170 dead pets in recent weeks

By crowdsourcing intel in this way, and collaborating with authorities, LAFS has so far uncovered a total of 15 dumpsites, containing an estimated 170 animals. "Some of them were just bags of bones," Chris says. "Others were liquefied."

LAFS has meticulously documented the crime scenes. Often wearing little more for protection than an N95 mask and rubber gloves, Chris crouches in the underbrush and slices open discarded plastic bags, picking through the contents while Geraldine holds the camera.

Their Facebook videos have racked up more than 30 million views. Commenters ask Chris if he has seen their pet among the bodies, or if he can turn a carcass over so they can check for identifying marks.

Some are successful. On that first night in October, all 25 bodies were returned to their owners.

There's a question which hangs uncomfortably over every dumpsite: who would do something like this?

As more owners came forward, a common denominator emerged. Many said they'd taken the animals to be incinerated at the same place: a crematorium called Ahjay Paws in the city of Bacoor, not far from Manila.

"So I posted: anybody that's had Ahjay's services... these might be your animals," says Chris. "And man, it just flooded in."

Ahjay Paws, the company that was supposed to cremate Olaf, is under investigation

The Philippines has long been a dog-loving nation, but since the Covid-19 pandemic the "fur parenting" community has exploded.

A 2021 study found the country had the highest rate of dog ownership in the Asia-Pacific. Since then, the pet dog population has increased 4.7% year on year, according to another study, exceeding 21 million in 2025.

As the numbers soar, businesses have stepped up to address an inevitable implication: more deaths.

"Cremation here in the Philippines has become quite a big thing with the boom of the pet industry," says Anna Cabrera, director at the Philippine Animal Welfare Society (Paws). "There's even a ceremony where they hold wakes for the pets."

These events offer grieving owners a last chance to see their beloved pets before they're taken to a crematorium and transmuted into an urnful of ashes.

Pet care - including for those that have died - has become a booming industry in the Philippines

Revecca Calling, who lives south of Bulacan in the city of Cavite, says she paid Ahjay Paws 8,500 Philippine pesos (Â£100; $133) to cremate her dog Olaf in January. The price was calculated according to weight, and included a 30-minute viewing where she could say her final goodbye.

Calling says Ahjay Paws told her they couldn't record the cremation, as it might endanger the staff. They took away Olaf's body, and a few days later handed back a small urn that purportedly contained her cremated remains.

When nine months later Calling heard that dozens of animals' bodies had been found dumped on an empty plot of land, potentially at the behest of a pet cremation business, she was devastated.

"Pet owners entrust these services with something very important: the remains of a beloved family member," she says. "We need to know what happened."

Pets have been found dumped in bags like this - with labels giving their names and weights

Earlier this month, the Philippines' National Bureau of Investigation (NBI) filed criminal complaints against four individuals in connection with dumped pets. Among them is the owner of Ahjay Paws and two people who ran Bulacan's Pet HPHT Crematory.

According to the NBI, Ahjay Paws had initially arranged to acquire a cremation machine from Pet HPHT â but when it didn't arrive, they opted to send the pet remains to Pet HPHT instead. When the volume of those remains became more than Pet HPHT could handle, that firm paid a third party to bury them.

One interpretation is that the dumping incidents are a result of logistical failures: two businesses that bit off more than they could chew and took desperate measures to make the problem go away.

But there's another theory: that the dead pets could be evidence of a lucrative racket, with people's money taken to incinerate their pets but the animals then being dumped instead.

There is a word for this kind of deception in Philippine law â estafa; where a perpetrator knowingly misleads a victim to financially defraud them â and the NBI say they are investigating it as a possibility in this case.

Two weeks ago, Ahjay Paws' owner denied responsibility for the dumping in San Jose del Monte, according to local media. The company is apparently no longer operating, and the BBC has been unable to contact it.

The BBC has also reached out to Pet HPHT and invited it to respond. On Facebook, the firm said the dumping allegations were "completely false and have no basis".

The pet-dumping scandal, in which Olaf may have been a victim, has blown up into a national controversy

Another grieving pet owner, Emelia Legada, says she paid Ahjay Paws 10,000 Philippine pesos (Â£120; $160) to cremate her dog Harvey six months ago.

"I just can't accept the pain of not knowing what might have happened to him," she says. "I really want to confirm whether Harvey was properly cremated, and whether the ashes I received were truly his."

Many are wrestling with the same uncertainty. As Cabrera puts it: "God knows what that was inside those urns."

In response to those anxieties, a veterinarian in nearby Quezon City has started offering complimentary forensic ash testing. For those whose pets have been found among the bodies, other crematoriums have offered to do the job properly, and for free.

The animal welfare society, Paws, is volunteering legal services to help people file complaints with the authorities, and has received more than 150 requests so far. It is also calling for a nationwide audit of all pet cremation businesses.

On 2 November, Percival Vilar CendaÃ±a of the Philippines' progressive Akbayan Party appeared on national television. He was there to speak about the pet-dumping scandal, which, in the span of a few days, had flared into a national disgrace.

"Our worst fears have been confirmed," he told the Bilyonaryo news channel. "Now we see that these are not isolated incidents. It is true that this is a syndicate that is conducting widespread operations."

Neither the police nor the NBI have confirmed that the dumpings were carried out by a "syndicate", nor that they were "widespread". Many details surrounding the case remain murky. But the hysteria is real â and CendaÃ±a was giving voice to a common sentiment.

"Those who deceived these poor fur parents should be jailed," he said.

At least three individuals have surrendered to the NBI and been charged with violating the city's Code of Sanitation, the Ecological Solid Waste Management Act, and the Sanitation Code of the Philippines.

But many feel this doesn't go far enough, and say it fails to hold anyone liable for the way the animals' remains were treated.

Cabrera says the country's animal-cruelty law doesn't cover the treatment of animals after death. And "there's no crime if there's no law".

The scandal has prompted a groundswell to change that. Lawmakers like CendaÃ±a are pushing for a national commission on animal welfare to ensure their dignity in life and death.

Meanwhile, Cabrera says Paws will seek "moral and exemplary" damages on behalf of aggrieved pet owners.

Chris Taylor dealing with items resembling clothing found at a dumpsite (blurred at source)

One woman whose dog was suspected to have been found among the dumped bodies told the BBC she couldn't speak about it because she was "feeling weak".

"I haven't slept for three days from all the crying," she said.

Chris has also been losing sleep, taking calls from grieving pet owners who have watched his videos. "Hundreds of people call me in tears, just sobbing," he says.

"They're in so much pain. And half the time you're telling them: 'you're not going to find your pet. It's been too long; we're not going to be able to identify it.' And it just crushes them."

As well as new laws, Chris wants stricter enforcement of existing rules. He wants to know how he managed to unearth so much in the space of a fortnight, and why people keep coming to him saying they don't trust the police to take their concerns seriously, he says.

The BBC contacted Philippine National Police (PNP) for comment, but didn't hear back. Earlier this month, PNP chief Jose Melencio Nartatez Jr said authorities were determined to hold accountable those who allegedly took advantage of grieving pet owners, saying such exploitation "must be addressed with the full force of the law", according to ABS-CBN.

"Fraud is not measured solely by the amount of money taken, but also by the damage inflicted on the people whose trust has been betrayed," Nartatez said.

"To the affected pet owners, we recognise the pain and distress this incident has caused your families."

Yet the police themselves have come under official scrutiny.

On Monday, the National Police Commission announced an investigation into officers in Cavite and Bulacan after they suggested LAFS might be involved in the pet dumping because they were often first on the scene. The officers did not present evidence for their claims. The investigation also covers reports that officers visited the Taylors' home and took photographs.

Meanwhile, countless grieving pet owners are suspended in purgatory: desperate for closure on what happened to their dogs and cats, and calling on authorities to do more.

"We want the case to be properly investigated and those responsible to be held accountable," says Calling.â¯ "I believe that no secret remains hidden forever."

Legada, for her part, says: "I just want to find out what really happened to Harvey and where his remains are. I want to know whether he was properly cremated."

She also wants people to know: "You can't trust anyone these days."

With additional reporting by Virma Simonette Rivera

#136. Eight injured after driver in Israel repeatedly rams car into Palestinians

Arab MPs accuse politicians of fuelling hostility as footage shows driver accelerating towards Palestinians at four locations in Kafr Qasim

Eight people were injured on Wednesday evening when a driver struck Palestinian residents at four separate locations in Kafr Qasim, a city in central Israel.

Footage shared on social media appears to show the car accelerating towards people, all Palestinians, at several locations in the city, striking them before driving away. Three of those injured were in moderate condition, and several of the victims were women leaving a nearby wedding, the Times of Israel reported.

Haaretz has reported that investigators are examining whether the driver struck people deliberately. Avi Levy, a police district commander, said the incident appeared to be a hit-and-run and that the driver had tested positive for drugs. He said the Shin Bet security service was taking part in the investigation and said: “As of this moment, nothing is coming up. The suspect has no criminal record.”

The incident prompted Arab lawmakers and activists to accuse Israeli politicians of fuelling hostility towards Arab citizens. Benjamin Netanyahu has called Arab parties “terror supporters” and said partnering with them would put Israel’s security at risk. The parties won a record 19 seats in the Knesset in the country’s recent election.

Yousef Jabareen, the chair of the Joint List, said the incident “raises difficult questions about the claim that the driver lost control”. He said: “I’ve been warning for weeks that the incitement against Arab citizens will turn into assaults and even assassinations.”

Said Issa, a community activist in Kafr Qasim, described the incident as an “act of terror”. “Someone who loses control of his vehicle might hit one or two vehicles, but not multiple people in several different places,” Issa said. “The attacker struck at four locations, one after the other.”

In a separate incident in the occupied West Bank, the Palestinian foreign ministry called on Monday for an Israeli settler to be held accountable after eight Palestinians were run over and injured in Tuqu, south-east of Bethlehem. Local sources told the Anadolu news agency that the driver had first struck three young men, then returned and ran over five others who had gathered to help them.

#135. Mexican special forces arrest drug kingpin 'Chapo Isidro'

The alleged leader of the Beltran Leyva gang and one of the FBI's most wanted fugitives, known as "Chapo Isidro," was captured by the Mexican military following a raid and a shootout in the coastal state of Sinaloa.

A gun battle between Mexican soldiers and alleged gang members in the city of Guasave, in the state of Sinaloa, ended with special forces detaining senior members of the Beltran Leyva drug trafficking network, Mexican officials said on Saturday.

The gang is aligned with the Mayos, one of the factions of the powerful Sinaloa cartel.

Authorities said the overnight raid resulted in the capture of the suspected drug kingpin Fausto Isidro Meza F., better known as "Chapo Isidro," who is believed to be the leader of Beltran Leyva.

The operation left at least 11 people dead. Seven alleged cartel gunmen and four Mexican soldiers were killed, Mexico’s Defense Ministry said on Saturday afternoon.

The 44-year-old Mexican national is one of the FBI's ten most wanted fugitives. The US law enforcement agency claims his group is responsible for smuggling "large quantities of heroin, methamphetamine, cocaine, fentanyl, and marijuana into the United States," and has offered a $5 million (€4.56 million) reward for information leading to his arrest.

He is also wanted in Mexico on charges including organized crime. "Chapo Isidro" was once a rival of imprisoned drug kingpin Joaquin "El Chapo" Guzman.

The three detainees were transported to Mexico City "to be placed at the disposal of the competent prosecutorial authorities," the military said.

It was not immediately clear whether authorities would extradite "Chapo Isidro" to the US.

On Saturday, Mexican authorities said the raid was conducted due to "intelligence coordination" between Mexico's military and US agencies. However, they offered few details about how authorities in both countries coordinated.

In a post on X, US Ambassador to Mexico Ronald Johnson wrote: "Results like these are not achieved overnight. They are the product of sustained cooperation and trust. This is what we can accomplish when we work together. ​We will continue pursuing criminals."

The troops used "stealth and surprise" to reach the area, establishing a perimeter around the drug dealer's location and then moving in from multiple directions, according to Mexico's US embassy.

Unverified videos from Guasave, however, showed military helicopters firing at ground targets and sports fans scrambling for safety during a baseball game. Some of the violence in the western Mexican city seems to have taken place after "Chapo Isidro" was arrested, with drug cartels attempting to retaliate against the authorities and civilians for the move.

Mexico's Defense Ministry confirmed cartel members set up roadblocks in the area, but added that security forces were assisting local authorities in restoring order.

The Guasave raid is only the latest in a string of high-profile moves against senior leaders of Mexico's illegal drug trade​​​​​​. Less than two weeks ago, the Mexican Navy announced the arrest of Rafael A., suspected of leading a cartel cell active in the states of Jalisco and Michoacan.

Mexican President ​Claudia Sheinbaum is facing pressure from Donald Trump, who has threatened to take military action against cartels on Mexican territory.

#133. ANC fights to keep control of South Africa's key cities after election setback

The ANC got the most votes overall but lost control in more than half of the councils it won in 2021

The party that has dominated South Africa for more than 30 years is in a race against time to secure power-sharing deals in most of the country's cities after a massive drop in its support in this month's local elections.

The African National Congress (ANC) suffered its worstâ¯result since it came to power in 1994 when South Africa transitioned from the racist apartheid system to democracy.

It won a majority in just one of the country's nine provinces, Limpopo, down from six in the last elections. It won the most votes in just two of the country's eight cities, compared with six in 2021.

At the national level, the ANC is sharing power with its long-time rival, the Democratic Alliance (DA), but while there is still no official announcement, it appears that ANC party officials do not want to replicate this at the local level.

Parties have until 27 November to negotiate and form local governments.

While the ANC saw a massive decline in its support, it still got more votes than any other party, 31%, down from the 46% in the last local election.

But in the country's economic powerhouse, Gauteng province, which includes Johannesburg and Pretoria, the picture is different. The pro-business DA got the most votes in both cities, as well as the province's other main region, Ekurhuleni.

Nationally, the party got 25% of the vote, up from 22% five years ago.

It also retained its majority in its power base of Cape Town, and got the most votes in Nelson Mandela Bay, where Gqeberha (Port Elizabeth) is located.

The left-leaning uMkhonto weSizwe (MK) Party, led by former President Jacob Zuma, came third, with 10% of the vote. This is a repeat of its performance in the 2024 national election, when it came in third and became the official opposition after the DA joined the coalition government.

It emerged as the biggest party in KwaZulu-Natal province, which includes the port city of Durban, securing 34% of the vote. However, this leaves it reliant on agreeing a coalition with other parties.

The only party to secure an overall majority in a city was the DA in Cape Town.

The DA and ANC have had an uneasy alliance since they entered a coalition, along with eight other political parties, in 2024.

The two, long-standing rivals due to their opposing policies, were forced into an agreement after the ANC lost its parliamentary majority for the first time since the end of apartheid. This became known as the Government of National Unity (GNU).

Despite this, the two parties have continued to disagree on key issues, and both sides have threatened to rip up their power-sharing deal.

Ahead of the local elections, analysts predicted that the two parties could team up at municipal level, mirroring the national arrangement.

ActionSA leader Herman Mashaba (left) and ANC chairperson Gwede Mantashe (right) look set to reach on agreement on co-governing some of South Africa's biggest cities

The ANC seems to have rejected this, with state broadcaster SABC reporting that most of the party's top leaders see an agreement with the DA as a last resort.

Instead, the party has been courting smaller parties to try and form councils in South Africa's cities.

In Gauteng, numerous reports point to an arrangement between the party and the anti-immigrant ActionSA, which emerged as the third largest party in the province with 17%, leaving it as a potential kingmaker.

The DA has also been trying to reach arrangements with smaller parties, and the ANC, but looks set to assume the opposition benches at least in Gauteng.

ActionSA is unlikely to work with the DA as they cannot agree on the mayoral candidates in Johannesburg and Pretoria.

It's too early to tell. Neither the ANC nor DA have spoken publicly about the future of their arrangement on a national level amid the ongoing coalition talks but the ANC's reluctance to partner with the DA in key cities points to continued strain between the two.

While the GNU has remained intact since it was formed in 2024, there have been those in the ANC who have been angered by what they view as the DA's dual role as opposition and partner in the coalition government and have pushed for the arrangement to end.

A party insider told the Daily Maverick on Sunday that a nationwide agreement to work with the DA at municipal level "was overwhelmingly rejected" at a meeting held by ANC members.

While some experts predict that the DA may have to consider its position in the coalition government given the ANC's stance, political analyst Andre Duvenhage told the BBC there was a possibility that "a lot of asymmetrical coalitions" could emerge - parties working together in one area and being in opposition elsewhere.

Another political analyst, Sandile Swana, said that any party wanting to leave the GNU over the failure to secure deals locally would be "[shooting] yourself in the foot".

The decline in the ANC's support was seen as being mainly due to high levels of corruption and a loss of confidence in its ability to provide basic services, such as electricity, water and rubbish collection.

While some attribute the increase backing for the DA to its record of competent management in municipalities under its control, some have accused it of neglecting poorer areas and townships.

But the experience of coalition governments over the past 10 years does not inspire much confidence that things will improve.

Half of the country's cities have been governed through power-sharing agreements since 2016 and these types of arrangements have often been viewed as unstable and chaotic.

As agreements collapse and new ones are forged, the delivery of basic services has often been affected.

This has been particularly evident in Gauteng, where governance in all three cities has come under scrutiny in recent years partly because of unstable governments.

Experts agree that even after new arrangements take effect, it will take longer than a five-year term to resolve the issues facing many municipalities, particularly South Africa's biggest city Johannesburg.

"I think if we are really turning the ship around, we are talking about a 10-year period," Duvenhage said.

Swana echoed this, saying it would take a decade to "cleanse and purify" local councils and "reinstate them as "proper municipalities".

ANC under pressure to reverse South Africa decline

'Devastating message' as ANC records worst-ever result in South Africa election

#132. Israeli tanks cross ‘yellow line’ boundary into Hamas-controlled central Gaza

First incursion across boundary since ceasefire deal comes as Eisenkot seeks to form government without Netanyahu

The Israeli military crossed into Hamas-controlled central Gaza on Wednesday in its first ground operation across the “yellow line” boundary since a fragile ceasefire was agreed last October, forcing dozens of displaced Palestinians to flee their tents.

Israeli tanks rolled along the Salah al-Din road, Gaza’s main north-south route, a day after the US-backed Board of Peace, a body overseeing the ceasefire, accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities.

An Israeli military spokesperson said troops were carrying out an operation targeting “Hamas infrastructure” west of the yellow line in central Gaza.

The operation lasted about 24 hours and on Thursday the military said it had been “completed”.

The IDF ​has not commented extensively on the operation, which came as the Israeli opposition politician, Gadi Eisenkot, has been seeking to form a government without the caretaker prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, who has built his political reputation on promises to keep Israel safe.

A Gaza security source told AFP there was “intense Israeli artillery shelling” north of Nuseirat refugee camp and reported Israeli military vehicles in central Gaza “amid ongoing gunfire and airstrikes since midday”.

Hamas condemned the Israeli raid and the forced displacement of Palestinians as “a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement” and a dangerous escalation. The Israeli military did not immediately respond to Hamas’s statement.

Residents reported hearing tank fire in the area. Palestinian medics said one person was killed in an Israeli airstrike near the site of the raid.

After the statement from the IDF that it had concluded the tank raid to dismantle a suspected Hamas tunnel, the defence ministry’s body in charge of civilian affairs in Palestinian territories, Cogat, said Palestinians would again be free to move in the targeted areas near al-Bureij and Nuseirat refugee camps.

In separate incidents on Wednesday, Palestinian medics said two people were killed by Israeli fire, one in Deir al-Balah and the other in Gaza City. The Israeli military said it was unaware of any incident in Deir al-Balah and had struck a militant in Gaza City.

Israel has stepped up its attacks on Hamas, which it says has been regrouping and rearming. Hamas has accused Israel of trying to derail the US-brokered ceasefire agreed in October 2025.

Israeli strikes have continued since the ceasefire, and Hamas remains armed.

Last week, an Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in southern Gaza killed six people, including a surgeon and a disabled man, and wounded 28 others, according to the hospital director.

The body overseeing Donald Trump’s plan to end the war in Gaza has accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities, leaving Palestinians in dire conditions and the ceasefire at risk of collapse.

In its second report to the UN security council since the truce began 13 months ago, the Trump-led Board of Peace said the gap between what was agreed and what was happening on the ground remained the biggest threat to the deal. The report, seen by Reuters, warned that “a ceasefire that delivers nothing visible cannot be sustained indefinitely”.

Gaza’s health authorities say at least 1,600 Palestinians have been killed since the ceasefire came into effect. The Israeli military says five of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

Palestinian authorities say more than 74,000 people have been killed in Gaza since Israel launched its military campaign in response to the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, in which about 1,200 people were killed.

In June, an ⁠independent UN inquiry determined that Israel was continuing to commit genocide by deliberately targeting Palestinian children. A previous report by the commission in September 2025 found that Israel had committed genocide in Gaza, and Israeli officials, including the prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, incited those acts. Netanyahu is separately wanted by the international criminal court (ICC) for war crimes.
