#103. International UN talks on controlling use of autonomous weapons collapse

Week-long efforts fail to establish negotiating mandate on AI warfare after Russian ‘filibustering’

Week-long efforts to agree a negotiating mandate for a legally binding international treaty to control the use of autonomous weapons collapsed just before 5am after all-night talks failed to overcome Russian objections.

Despite an overwhelming majority of states at the UN’s Convention on Certain Conventional Weapons (CCW) in Geneva wanting to agree a negotiating mandate leading to a legally binding treaty, the need for consensus forced the conference to adopt a much weaker option.

The mandate asked countries “to elaborate by consensus”, an instrument that may not carry any legal obligations.

Although Russia led the objections using obstructive stalling tactics, other military powers invested in AI warfare, such as the US, were reluctant to commit to a legally binding protocol protecting civilians from robot drones and other autonomous weapons.

Some argued that international humanitarian law was sufficient. More than 100 states had been pushing for a new law, saying the weapons raised entirely new challenges. European and Nato states, including Ukraine, China and Iran, expressed support or willingness for a new protocol.

The US said the need for human control over autonomous weapons had been discussed at the Apec summit in meetings between president Xi Jinping and Donald Trump this week, but the US was reluctant to agree a binding treaty.

South Korea, Japan, Turkey, the UK, France and Germany were among the defence tech powers exploring AI-enabled weapons who also expressed resistance to being bound by a new law, instead advocating for a nonbinding agreement.

The CCW is a UN forum that operates via consensus, meaning all 128 parties to the convention have to agree unanimously to pass a statement. The high threshold has resulted in the CCW failing to pass any new laws regulating weapons in the past 30 years and states seeking pathways elsewhere to pass treaties, such as the 2008 ban on cluster munitions.

The Future of Life Institute’s chair, Max Tegmark, said world leaders had failed to meet the moment this week. “Slaughterbots are already here,” he said. “Yet states have agreed it is sufficient to just talk about this for the 14th year.”

Diplomats inside the negotiations said Russia had been deliberately filibustering and not working in good faith, as opposed to the US delegates, who had been seeking a path to reach a consensus statement.

The exclusion of several nonmember countries from negotiations also prompted a rare protest in the chamber on the final day of the conference, with the Egyptian delegation shouting at the conference chair.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

Israel and the US have also used AI-enabled targeting systems to conduct tens of thousands of strikes in Gaza and Iran, which have resulted in mass casualties. The Pentagon struck more than 1,000 targets in Iran on the first day of the war, and according to investigative media reports, the US military is aiming to carry out 1,000 AI-assisted targeting decisions per hour.

Critics said this outcome was a missed opportunity, noting that states had been discussing the issue of autonomous weapons, their dangers, and the need for international rules on how to restrict and regulate them for 13 years and large substantive parts of a treaty had already been settled upon.

One European diplomat said: “There was no political will to really regulate these weapons. No will commensurate to the urgency and need.”

A Human Rights Watch’s senior arms adviser, Bonnie Docherty, who had been directly involved in previously successful treaties banning cluster munitions and nuclear weapons, said autonomous weapons needed binding law. She noted that treaties could be negotiated within 15 months and dismissed the US’s proposal of five years to sort out a mandate.

A bloc of seven countries – Austria, Belgium, Brazil, Ireland, Mexico, New Zealand and Panama – had led the push for a binding treaty to be negotiated within one year.

On Wednesday Brazil’s delegate told the floor: “These are not weapons of the future being discussed here, these are already in use and being further developed. We are late in developing these norms and rules to regulate these lethal autonomous weapons systems.”

#101. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#99. Fred Nile, former NSW politician and figurehead of the rightwing Christian Democratic party, dies aged 92

Controversial figure frequently took positions against drug use, abortion and homosexuality, but in later years supported legislation expunging homosexual convictions

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

The former New South Wales MP and longstanding figurehead of the rightwing Christian Democratic party, the Rev Fred Nile, has died aged 92.

Nile was a member of the NSW legislative council for more than 40 years. He first entered the state’s upper house in 1981 and retired after unsuccessfully contesting the 2023 election.

Nile was a controversial figure; a standard-bearer for Christian and social conservative politics since long before the formation of the Australian Christian Lobby and other faith-based political movements.

“It is with deep sadness the Nile family announces the passing of Rev. the Hon. Frederick John Nile who passed away peacefully on the 21st of November at the age of 92,” his family said in a statement.

“Rev. Nile is survived by his wife, children, grandchildren and great grandchildren.

“Details about a service or memorial will be shared soon.”

Nile, an ordained Congregational Union minister, and the political party he led for decades frequently took positions against drug use, abortion and homosexuality.

Alex Greenwich, the member for Sydney, said that despite disagreeing on “a lot” with Nile, the pair had a good working relationship, particularly in relation to protecting First Nations cultural sites.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

“Rev. Nile also played a key role in passing legislation for the expungement of prior homosexual convictions, landmark laws that cleared the names of hundreds of gay men,” Greenwich said.

“Rev. Nile played the ball, not the person. His opposition to many of the reforms I delivered came from his faith, and he didn’t engage in the personalised vitriol that comes from many of today’s right-wing politicians.”

Greenwich said he had asked the premier, Chris Minns, for a state funeral for Nile.

Lyle Shelton, who had initially been chosen by Nile as his successor to lead the Christian Democratic party in 2021 before the pair had a falling out, said Nile “stood in the public square for Christian ethics and moral virtues at time when few would”.

“Cruelly caricatured by his opponents, anyone who met him knew the caricature of the mockers did not fit,” Shelton said.

After US President Donald Trump called for UFO files to be released in an executive order earlier this year, the Pentagon has declassified hundreds of files. The first tranche was released 8 May 2026.

The footage in this video makes up three of the 43 files released in this tranche. All three clips were filmed from aboard US military platforms in the Middle East in 2020, 2023 and 2026.

#95. Indian man sentenced to 19-plus years in plot to assassinate US-based Sikh separatist leader

Watch: Inside court as Nikhil Gupta sentenced to 19 years over assassination plot

A federal judge sentenced an Indian man to 235 months, more than 19 years, in an attempted plot to kill a leading Sikh political figure in the US.

Nikhil Gupta, an Indian national, pleaded guilty to murder-for-hire, conspiracy to commit murder-for-hire, and conspiracy to commit money laundering in February. On Friday, the judge said he likely will be deported or removed after his sentence.

US prosecutors alleged Gupta was directed by an Indian government official to kill Gurpatwant Singh Pannun, a leading figure of the Khalistan movement, which advocates for an independent homeland for Sikhs.

India has denied involvement. Its government has accused Pannun of being a terrorist, which he denies.

Pannun, an American citizen, is the spokesman for US-based Sikh separatist group Sikhs for Justice (SFJ), banned in India since 2019.

He previously told the BBC that "Nikhil Gupta's guilty plea was judicial confirmation that India's Modi government orchestrated a structured murder-for-hire assassination plot on American soil".

India has opposed the Khalistan movement, and its political parties also remain opposed to the matter. Sikhs are a religious minority that make up about 2% of India's population. Some groups have long called for a separate homeland for Sikhs.

Nikhil Gupta (seen in a 2024 courtroom sketch) pleaded guilty in a plot to kill Gurpatwant Singh Pannun, a U.S. resident who advocated for a sovereign Sikh state in northern India

Prosecutors alleged that in May 2023, Gupta was recruited by Vikash Yadav, an Indian intelligence officer, to carry out an assassination of Pannun.

Yadav allegedly provided Gupta with personal information about Pannun, including his address in New York and information about his daily activities, prosecutors said.

At his handler's direction, Gupta contacted someone to facilitate the assassination, but that person was actually a US government informant, prosecutors said. The informant introduced Gupta to a "hitman", who was actually an undercover officer with the Drug Enforcement Agency.

Gupta was arrested in June 2023 at an airport in Prague, Czech Republic and extradited to the US. He has been held at a jail in New York pending his sentencing Friday at a Manhattan court.

"He thought that from outside this country he could kill someone in it without consequence, simply for exercising their American right to free speech," then-US attorney Jay Clayton said in a statement, after announcing Gupta's guilty plea.

Following Gupta's arrest, a spokesperson for India's Ministry of External Affairs said that Yadav is no longer employed, external by the Indian government.

The country also set up an inquiry committee to address security concerns raised by the US, the spokesperson said.

Narinder Singh Randhawa, a Sikh separatist and successor of Hardeep Singh Nijjar as temple leader in Surrey, British Columbia, told the BBC the sentencing marks the "first time we see justice" against what he calls historic and systemic persecution of Sikhs by the Indian government in India and abroad.

Inderjeet Gosal, another Sikh separatist advocate based in Ontario, called the sentencing "a big community event".

"It's for all the community to finally witness someone getting caught red-handed."

Indian man admits plot to kill US-based Sikh separatist leader

Who was Canadian Sikh leader Hardeep Singh Nijjar?

Why are some Sikhs calling for a separate state?

Canada accuses India of role in Sikh leader's murder

India crushed a violent armed Sikh insurgency in the 1980s and 1990s aimed at demanding the creation of Khalistan. The movement has little support in India now although some in the Sikh diaspora continue to advocate for Khalistan.

Pannun is facing serious charges, including in connection with terrorism, in India, but he denies the allegations, saying he is an activist who just believes in Khalistan.

In a victim impact statement to the court, Pannun said the foiled assassination attempt has not deterred his activism.

"I am entirely willing to take India's bullets if that is the price required to achieve freedom," he wrote.

Gupta's case placed increased scrutiny on a separate incident, involving the 2023 murder of a Sikh separatist leader in British Columbia, which led to increased tensions between India and Canada.

In September 2023, then-Canadian Prime Minister Justin Trudeau said there were "credible allegations potentially linking" India to the murder of Hardeep Singh Nijjar, another advocate for the creation of Khalistan and an associate of Pannun's.

Nijjar was shot and killed by two assailants outside a Sikh temple in British Columbia. India also has denied involvement in his death.

The day after Niijar's murder, US prosecutors allege that Gupta told the undercover officer Nijjar "was also the target" and "we have so many targets."

#94. ‘No time for a culture war’: Victoria’s electricity supply is at risk. Can it avoid power shortages and bill shocks?

State faces electricity supply gap when Yallourn coal-fired power station closes in 2028 as experts warn neither Labor nor the Coalition has adequate plans

Sign up for climate and environment editor Adam Morton’s free Clear Air newsletter here

Victoria’s electricity supply will be at risk when a major power plant shuts in 2028 and neither major party is offering a plan at this month’s state election to deal with it, experts have warned.

All parties concede the giant Yallourn coal plant, which has operated in the Latrobe Valley since the 1970s and supplies a fifth of Victoria’s energy, is at the end of its functional life and will shut in less than two years. Analysts say the parties vying to run the state are failing to properly explain how they will fill the power shortfall the closure will create.

The challenge facing Victoria is exacerbated by the growth in energy demand from datacentres and the electrification of buildings, homes and vehicles. Experts are most critical of the Coalition, under Liberal leader Jess Wilson, which is favoured to win the election. It says it would stop work on new transmission lines – the high-voltage power lines needed to connect new wind and solar farms and allow Victoria to import power from neighbouring states.

Tim Baxter, of consultancy Naru Research, says there is no “time for a culture war on energy in Victoria” because poor choices now would be “devastating” for the state’s economy and create bill shocks for households and businesses.

Sign up to get climate and environment editor Adam Morton’s Clear Air column as a free newsletter

He says cheap gas helped build Victoria’s past prosperity, but it is quickly running out with offshore gas supply from Bass Strait in steep decline. And there is no evidence the state has meaningful gas resources elsewhere to replace it. This has contributed to a 62% increase in gas prices in five years, he says, while electricity bills have risen by 18%.

Given Victorians rely more on gas than other states, Baxter says it underlines the need for households to move away from gas and increasingly run on renewable energy. “Every windfarm, solar farm and battery that comes online before 2028 reduces how much expensive gas Victoria has to burn when Yallourn goes. That makes speeding up the build-out the single best way to protect households from higher bills,” he says.

Victoria’s wholesale electricity prices are the lowest on the east coast, but households still rely heavily on expensive gas. Regardless of who wins, energy bills are likely to increase, according to a government briefing reported in the Herald Sun.

The Coalition says it would scrap VNI West, a transmission line linking Victoria to New South Wales, and pause work on the Western Renewables Link, between Melbourne and the state’s west. Instead, it would undertake a “short, sharp and independent” review of the state’s transmission plan, which would consider alternatives, such as solar power on commercial and industrial rooftops.

It has also pledged to “throw open the doors to gas exploration”, reverse Labor’s policy of making new homes all-electric, and tender for new firming capacity with an Energy Security Requirement modelled on schemes in NSW and South Australia. There is no plan to change the state’s emission reduction targets – cutting greenhouse gas emissions by 75%–80% below 2005 levels by 2035 and reaching net zero emissions by 2045 – but it has not said how it would achieve them.

Labor says it would continue to expand the power grid, shift homes from gas to electricity for heating and cooking and continue to support the development of offshore windfarms. But the Carroll government is also reviewing the Western Renewables Link. It recently cancelled a renewable energy zone, approved two gas developments (although one has since entered administration), and announced $7.5m to kickstart a “renewable gas industry”.

Nexa Advisory chief executive Stephanie Bashir says new transmission is crucial for developing renewable energy and allowing power to be imported across state lines at times of high demand, such as during heatwaves. “This is not the time to be cancelling any of the projects in the pipeline,” she says. “This is the time where we put our foot on the accelerator and go hard.”

Environment Victoria’s chief executive, Jono La Nauze, says the Coalition’s plan – including a proposal to reinstate a policy introduced by the previous Baillieu-Napthine Coalition government preventing wind turbines being built within 2km of dwellings – is “the greatest swindle ever attempted on Victorian voters”.

“The Coalition are promising to stop all the things that are necessary to replace our coal and gas generation. They’ve got some quick slogans for how they’re going to instead rely on gas, with absolutely no substance,” he says.

But analysts say Labor is also not building energy infrastructure fast enough.

Adam Morton brings you incisive analysis about the politics and impact of the climate crisis

Oliver Nunn, managing director of the consultancy Endgame Analytics, says the lack of an adequate plan to stave off an energy shortfall means that “whoever wins [the election], we lose”.

“You can see demand for electricity pulling up rapidly in Victoria, 5% year-on-year growth,” Nunn says. “Coal’s going out, and we just do not have, at this point, a really viable plan.”

In its 12 years in government, Labor has overseen a surge in Victoria’s share of renewable energy from 10% to 44% and the closure of the large Hazelwood coal-fired power station. The number of windfarms has grown from 14 to 44 and large-scale solar farms from two to 57. Labor also ended the state’s moratorium on conventional gas exploration in 2021.

But the state’s auditor general says both VNI West and the Western Renewables Link are behind schedule, and not likely to be completed until after Yallourn’s scheduled closure.

Recent attempts to fast-track the process by legislating for compulsory land acquisition before environmental approvals have been unpopular in regional communities, but Labor says the changes were needed to reduce delays and improve certainty for landowners. Controversially, Labor also passed laws to allow VicGrid and its contractors access to private land without a landholder’s consent, as a last resort to facilitate works.

“Every time the government seems to hit a hurdle, what they’ve done is not gone back and reassessed the engagement and tried to improve it, what they’ve done is changed legislation to give them broader powers,” the Victorian Farmers Federation president, Ryan Milgate, says.

The shadow energy minister, David Davis, refers to the laws as Labor’s “double bazooka powers” and says the Coalition will ditch them and reinstate landholder appeal rights.

But Nexa’s Bashir says energy policy should not be “a political football”.

“We do need to back the replacement generation. We can’t do any more reviews; we’ve just got to get on with it.”

#93. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#88. Trump threatens ban on US firms building AI data centres overseas

Trump says he won't allow American companies to build data centres "outside" the US

Donald Trump has said he will ban US companies from building AI data centres overseas, threatening a "tremendous tax" for tech firms who do so.

"We're going to keep it all in this country," the US president said, while providing little detail on the potential policy he said others in his cabinet were hearing about for the first time.

Data centres were a central issue in the midterm elections earlier in November, with many Democratic candidates promising tougher regulation on their construction.

Tech giants including Meta and Google have entered agreements to build data centres overseas. It remains unclear how such a policy would be enforced if it was brought in.

"We're not going to allow our companies to build data centres outside of our country," Trump told reporters in a press briefing following his return from his trip to Asia.

"Because China will take all of them, and we're not allowing that to happen."

But whether that idea will become policy remains unclear.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story, but I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now, they're here," he said.

Trump floated the policy after saying the US was leading on data centre construction, and he would not allow China to beat the US when it came to AI - which he calls super intelligence, or SI.

It also follows an announcement earlier this week that would restrict the export of advanced computer chips and who can access the computing power provided by them.

Washington on Monday announced firms would require a licence to buy advanced chips, so US officials can monitor whether they are being used to serve Chinese companies or they are being smuggled into China.

The move will impact South East Asian countries that have invested heavily in data centres, as Chinese companies seek to rent computing power from overseas.

Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Data centres are crucial to the AI boom. The vast warehouse-style buildings house the advanced computer chips and servers that are needed to run AI programmes such as Claude and ChatGPT.

As of April there were about 3,000 data centres across the US, with another 1,500 in various stages of approval and construction, according to the Pew Research Centre.

Many of them are built in rural areas, and concerns about issues including noise pollution and energy consumption helped drive Democratic candidates promising tougher rules on data centre construction to wins on 3 November.

"You ought to take advantage of the data centres. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but take advantage don't shut them off," Trump said.

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy and they have everything. They're tremendous assets."

Large tech firms - often called AI hyperscalers due to the size of their investments in AI - have also been branching out, and Canada has made moves to step in to offer itself as an alternative location for these sites, with less local pushback against projects so far.

Meta has already broken ground on a data centre, external in the province of Alberta, and Anthropic has signed a long-term agreement to lease a proposed data centre in Nova Scotia.

The BBC has reached out to Meta for comment on Trump's remarks.

In September Google announced a â¬13bn ($15bn: Â£11bn) investment in AI infrastructure in Finland, which included funding for three data centres.

Last month, building work for two of those centres was halted due to concerns over the clearance of more than 300 hectares of forest.

Trump called out the build in Finland specifically, saying his administration was "not allowing that to happen".

#87. Victorian Greens win injunction over how-to-vote cards mimicking party’s branding but preferencing it last

Exclusive: Independent candidate Tosh-Jake Finnigan’s cards instructed people to vote in a way that would benefit Liberal party

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

The Victorian Greens have won a supreme court injunction to stop an independent candidate from handing out how to vote cards that mimic their branding and urge people to preference it last.

The decision has been welcomed by the party’s leader, Ellen Sandell, who said the flyers distributed by people wearing the exact same colour shirts as genuine Greens volunteers had already misled voters.

“The last thing people need when they walk into a polling booth is dodgy how-to-vote material designed to trick them into voting the exact opposite way they intended,” Sandell said.

The decision comes after Guardian Australia confirmed that people handing out material for the independent candidate Tosh-Jake Finnigan were initially offered $25 an hour to do so.

As prepoll opened on Wednesday morning, Finnigan’s campaign team handed out how-to-vote cards that used the minor party’s colour scheme, font and triangle logo with the banner “Vote 1 Green Values”.

The card instructed people to vote in a way that would ultimately benefit the Liberal party. It asked voters to give their first preference to Finnigan followed by an Animal Justice party candidate, another independent candidate and then the Liberals in fourth place.

The Greens are determined to win back the ultra-marginal seat it lost to the Liberals at a February 2025 byelection with a 1.3% margin. Labor is running an open ticket in Prahran, which the Greens fear could deprive it of the preference votes it needs.

On Wednesday afternoon, the Greens state director, Martin Shield, requested Finnigan immediately stop distributing the material and threatened to take legal action if an undertaking was not given to do so in less than two hours.

Finnigan agreed to the request and produced an amended how-to-vote card within hours that included different colours with a revised banner that said “Vote 1 Trans Greens Values”.

“Due to legal threats from the Greens party, I confirm that I am not an endorsed Greens party candidate,” a disclaimer on Finnigan’s revised how-to-vote card said.

But the changes did not satisfy the Greens. On Friday afternoon, the party’s lawyers lodged papers in the supreme court.

At an initial hearing on Friday night, the Greens’ lawyer, Adam McBeth, alleged Finnigan’s team stood next to Greens party volunteers and Greens placards. He told the court the flyers were designed to mislead voters.

“There is no other reason for an independent candidate who has an infinite range of colours and styles to choose from to so closely mimic the Greens, including the way people paid to hand out were dressed and where they were directed to stand,” McBeth said.

Guardian Australia has confirmed an ad on Airtasker offering payment to those handing out flyers at pre-poll in Prahran was for Finnegan’s campaign.

“I need people to hand out flyers at election booths in Prahran and Richmond $25 per hour during the day from 18-28 November,” the ad said.

But the person who published the ad, Finnegan’s friend Dean Hurston, said no one was recruited through Airtasker as Finnegan had found volunteers elsewhere.

Justice John Watson granted the injunction on both how-to-vote cards on Sunday morning, after hearing testimony from the Victorian Electoral Commission on Saturday afternoon.

Finnigan, who was self-represented, said they had gone above and beyond to quickly make changes to their initial how-to-vote card and accused the Greens of using “legal manoeuvres” to stop them campaigning.

“It is my belief that they don’t want me to hand out on Saturday, which will be one of the busiest days of prepoll,” Finnigan said.

Finnigan told the court that they did not have enough money to print new cards. They said their campaign team had not been instructed to stand next to the Greens at prepoll.

“I am someone who holds Greens values, of course I am going to have some kind of green shirt,” Finnigan said.

“The honest reason I went for that colour of green is because the colour is called Irish green and I am of Irish background. That’s why I went for it. I just like the sound of Irish green.”

On Friday evening, after the supreme court action was lodged, the Victorian Election Commission (VEC) accepted Finnigan’s subsequent how-to-vote card and published it online.

Justice John Watson said the first how-to-vote card “looked like a pretty deliberate attempt to pass yourself off as a Greens candidate”, but he adjourned the matter until 3pm on Saturday to hear testimony from the VEC.

A decision on whether to grant an injunction will be made on Sunday morning.

Finnigan became Victoria’s first openly transgender councillor in 2023 when they were elected to the Colac Otway shire council after a countback. They resigned from the council in October 2024.

The former Labor field campaigner founded the Restore Democracy Sack Dan Andrews party in 2022. They unsuccessfully contested an upper house seat for the party, securing 1% of first preference votes.

#85. Zelensky says Putin is a 'killer' after Trump calls Russian leader a friend

Watch: Trump defends calling Putin a 'friend'

Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelensky has said Vladimir Putin is a "killer" in response to Donald Trump referring to the Russian leader as his "friend" in China this week.

Speaking to CNN, Zelensky said it was up to the US president "how to call Putin" but "for us, he is an enemy... for us, he's a killer".

Before Thursday's historic trilateral meeting with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Putin, Trump called Putin his "friend" and said the two leaders have had a "great relationship for many years".

Russian attacks continued overnight, with two people killed, including a boy, and 52 injured in missile and drone strikes on Zelensky's hometown of Kryvyi Rih in the central Dnipropetrovsk region, officials said.

Ukraine's state emergency service DSNS said 11 people were also injured in the north-eastern Kharkiv region, and one person in Rivne, in the north-west.

On Friday, at least nine people were killed in Russian strikes targeting bridges in the capital Kyiv and railway stations in western Ukraine - with Zelensky calling the attack the "biggest this year" after an onslaught of 110 missiles, including 27 ballistic, and more than 400 drones.

The strikes came days after Ukraine had badly damaged the Crimean Bridge.

Hours after Friday's strikes on Ukraine, Trump told reporters at the White House that Putin would "love to end the war".

"They [Zelensky and Putin] both want to end it and it's a very good thing I have a relationship," he said on Friday.

Trump also doubled down on his description of the Russian leader as a friend but said he "can't speak" to whether Putin is a friend to the US.

However Zelensky said despite Trump's stance, he saw Putin as "a killer".

"I think mostly for the people in the world, he is a killer because he's killing - by the way- he's killing each day and night," he told CNN.

Zelensky also said speaking about Putin like he was a "friend" meant "he doesn't feel enough pressure".

The war of words came as neighbouring Poland said a Russian cruise missile was among three objects that violated its airspace in the south-east of the country early on Friday.

Polish Defence Minister WÅadysÅaw Kosiniak-Kamysz said the missile hit a rural area at Nowy Lipowiec about 50km (31 miles) from the Ukrainian border.

This was about 100km from where European troops - from 11 of the 36 member nations of the coalition of the willing - were completing a training exercise, Polish authorities said. Another cruise missile and a jet-powered drone were suspected to have hit two other locations, they added.

On Saturday, Polish Foreign Minister RadosÅaw Sikorski told the BBC the cruise missiles had guidance systems, so "it is very unlikely that they veered off course as a result of Ukrainian electronic warfare".

"This is a war in Europe that affects our security," he told Radio 4's Today programme, also referencing similar incidents in recent months across Europe.

Nobody was hurt and no damage has been reported, but Warsaw described the incident as a "provocation" from Moscow.

Nato Secretary General Mark Rutte condemned "grave violations of Polish airspace" and said the military alliance had scrambled its jets in response.

Russia has made no comment about the violations, which took place during Friday's massive attack on Ukraine.

Putin launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.

#83. Man found dead on Flinders Island believed to have been shot by officer, police say

The sergeant, who was shot, fired his weapon a number of times after alleged ‘targeted’ attack outside island’s police station

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A police officer who was shot outside a remote Tasmanian police station is alleged to have shot and killed his attacker in response to what authorities believe was a “targeted” incident.

Tasmania police deputy commissioner, Jonathan Higgins, said the sergeant Michael Preshaw had just come off shift at Whitemark police station on Flinders Island, which is north-east of the Tasmanian mainland. He had parked and gotten out of his police vehicle when he was shot at about 9.45pm on Saturday, Higgins told reporters on Sunday.

Investigators sent to investigate the shooting discovered the body of a man, aged in his mid-20s, on Walker Street a few hundred metres from the police station.

Police believe he was shot by Preshaw, who fired his weapon a number of times.

A firearm and a balaclava were found at a separate location nearby, police said.

“At this early stage in the investigation, police believe this was a targeted incident,’” Higgins said.

Preshaw underwent surgery at the Royal Hobart Hospital on Sunday for injuries to his torso, police said, before being transferred to intensive care.

The 33-year police veteran is one of three officers stationed on Flinders Island and has worked there for a number of years.

Whitemark has a population of about 300 according to the 2021 census, and the entire population of Flinders Island is fewer than 1,000 people.

Extra police resources, including forensic and uniformed officers, have been deployed to the island. Those with information about these incidents, including CCTV or dashcam footage of Walker Street, have been urged to contact police.

Police are hoping to speak to the family of the deceased man.

Higgins said it was “the nature of our work” for police to receive threats from the public.

He reassured the Flinders Island community that the situation was safely contained, and said the death would be investigated by the coroner.

The Tasmanian premier, Jeremy Rockliff, has been in contact with the state’s police commissioner, Donna Adams, who attended the Hobart hospital as the shot sergeant undergoes treatment.

“My thoughts are with everyone affected by this horrific incident, including the police officer, their family and colleagues,” Rockliff said.

“Our police officers work every single day to keep Tasmanians safe, and incidents like this are a stark reminder of the risks they face in the line of duty.”

It is the second shooting of an on-duty police officer in Tasmania in 18 months. A Tasmanian officer was killed in the state’s north-west in June 2025 while executing a court-issued warrant to repossess a residence. The occupant of the home pleaded guilty to murder.

#78. Trafalgar Square Christmas tree felled in Norway

Karen Scarborough (left) and Anne Lindboe got to work with a two-handled saw

This year's Trafalgar Square Christmas tree has been cut down in a forest outside Oslo, ready to begin its journey to London.

The 21m (69ft) tree, nicknamed Jolly Cheery, is about 75 years old. It was felled on Friday by the Lord Mayor of Westminster, Karen Scarborough, and the Mayor of Oslo, Anne Lindboe.

Norway has sent a tree to the capital every year since 1947 as a thank you for Britain's support during World War Two, when King Haakon VII and the Norwegian government lived in exile in London.

Its lights will be switched on by the two mayors at a ceremony in the square on Thursday, 3 December.

Scarborough said: "It's my greatest honour to take part in this wonderful tradition for both the cities of Oslo and London.

"This tree is a powerful symbol of friendship and peace, reminding us to be there for one another during the festive period."

The 21m tree has been nicknamed Jolly Cheery

Scarborough thanked "the people of Oslo and Norway for this truly fabulous gift" and added: "With the care and dedication of the team, I'm sure it will look magnificent on Trafalgar Square."

Lindboe said: "Oslo has been sending a Christmas tree to London for almost eight decades, and every year we send a tree across the North Sea with the same message: we have not forgotten.

"The tradition has a powerful history, and its message remains just as relevant today.

"I hope Jolly Cheery spreads warmth and Christmas cheer to everyone who passes by, and reminds us how much it means to be there for one another, especially in difficult times."

Count the rings: Anne Lindboe (left) and Karen Scarborough (right) with a slice of the 75-year-old trunk

For the second year running, the tree was chosen with the help of a social media poll.

Organisers said it would travel by road in Norway and England using only electric and biodiesel vehicles, cutting the journey's carbon footprint by 80%. It will cross the North Sea on a regular cargo ship from the port of Brevik.

Once in London, it will be decorated in the traditional Norwegian style, with 25m strings of festoon lighting fitted with energy-efficient bulbs.

#75. Mamdani announces plan to close New York's Rikers Island jail by 2032

Perched on a 413 acre (167 hectare) island between Queens and the Bronx, the notorious Rikers Island jail complex earned a variety of nicknames over the years

New York City's official overseeing the closure of the city's most notorious jail hopes a new plan from Mayor Zohran Mamdani will lead to "a moment of transformation" for how people are detained in the largest US city.

Mamdani on Friday unveiled a plan to close the Rikers Island complex by 2032, to build four new jails and drastically reduce the city's detainee population.

Plans to shutter the troubled facility date back to 2019, when the city council first voted to close it by this year. But plans have been delayed over the years in part due to the logistical difficulties over where to place inmates.

Dana Kaplan - who Mamdani appointed as the city's "Close Rikers Czar" told the BBC News channel on Friday that his latest proposal is not just about getting rid of a decrepit facility.

It is also a "generational opportunity" to "build a different type of detention system" and figure out how to detain people more appropriately, she said.

The Rikers Island jail complex is perched on a 413 acre (167 hectare) island between Queens and the Bronx, and has earned a variety of nicknames over the years because of its dilapidated conditions, including Gladiator School and Torture Island. The jail has housed a number of high-profile inmates awaiting trial, including Tupac Shakur, DMX and Harvey Weinstein.

It houses more than 6,500 inmates at a given time. Mamdani hopes to reduce that number to 4,100 - a goal that has been met with skepticism by the New York City correction officers' union.

"[B]uilding a new system that puts an arbitrary cap on the City's available jail beds is ill-conceived, irresponsible and jeopardizes the safety of all New Yorkers, including our Officers," NYC Correction Officers' Benevolent Association said in a statement. "The City cannot keep the jail population at around 4,100 without irresponsibly releasing individuals who committed violent felony charges."

"It's actually possible to have fewer people in jail in New York City - and that is what we should be planning for," she said.

The jail complex has long faced backlash over staffing shortages, crumbling conditions and inmate deaths in the facility, including nine this year. Legislation required the jail to be shuttered by 2027, but Mamdani blamed lags under former Mayor Eric Adams' administration as well as the Covid pandemic for the delays.

Last year, under Adams, a federal judge removed Rikers from the city's control, appointing an independent outside official to make decisions concerning the facility.

In an 88-page report released on Friday, Mamdani said Rikers was a product of "deteriorating infrastructure" and that officials had a "moral imperative" to close it.

New York City Council must approve the updated timeline, with members, including the city's speaker, indicating they support the plan.

Mamdani's office said the city would open up four new jails - one in Brooklyn by 2029, two in the Bronx and Queens by 2031 and one in Manhattan by 2032.

The mayor's office argues the new, smaller facilities will provide inmates "access to daylight, fresh air and clean space" leading to better outcomes for detainees. The facilities will be easier for families to access as well, as travel to the remote Rikers can take hours.

Mamdani said his administration would work to reduce the number of people being held in jail across New York City, including by offering alternative programmes to detention for some awaiting trial, by making it easier for families to post bail online and by trying to speed up the pretrial legal process.

He also wants to open up hundreds of beds in therapeutic housing units for prisoners with serious mental health issues.

Kaplan said Mamdani's administration had already opened 100 therapeutic beds at Bellevue Hospital in Manhattan, moving 100 people off of Rikers Island.

The proposal involves a large investments: $146m (Â£111m) for mental health and substance use treatment and vocational and education programming inside and outside the jails.

#74. Police say man whose body lay undiscovered in Sydney bushland for 12 days was killed in suspected hit-and-run

Joshua Howard, 31, is believed to have been struck by a car and thrown over the guardrail as he was walking home from a party in Rouse Hill

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The twin brother of a man whose body lay undiscovered for 12 days after he was killed in a suspected hit and run in Sydney says it has left him feeling like half a person.

Joshua Howard was last seen at a bus interchange in Parramatta, in western Sydney, about 10pm on 7 November.

The 31-year-old was reported missing the following day and detectives later established he had been walking along Garfield Street East in Rouse Hill, in the city’s north-west, about 12.45 that morning.

An extensive search for began in the Rouse Hill area on Thursday with help from Blue Mountains Rescue, the New South Wales State Emergency Service and the Rural Fire Service.

His body was found by police in a bushland gully near Windsor Road in Rouse Hill about 2.45pm on Friday.

Superintendent Jason Joyce said police believe Howard was hit by a car as he was making his way home from a friend’s Diwali party.

“We believe he’s been walking along the side of the road and then been struck, and that’s propelled him over the guardrail into the bushland,” Joyce told reporters on Sunday.

“Based on his injuries … we believe there was some level of force of him being struck by the vehicle.”

It took police 11 days to start the land search because they were initially trying to track Howard’s movements through CCTV footage and doorknocking houses in the area, Joyce said.

Howard’s parents, Peter and Sue, described him as a loving son and sibling to his twin David and older brother Matthew.

He was a talented artist who graduated from the National Art School and always carried a sketchbook and pen with him, they said.

“When he was missing, David went through his room to see whether all those books were gone,” Sue Howard told reporters.

“But they were all there, indicating he was coming home that night.”

She said her family was heartbroken to learn they had likely driven past him the day after he was killed, without knowing his body was there.

“Our poor boy was there for 12 days,” she said.

She said David was particularly devastated, telling her: “I feel as if I’m half a person now.”

Peter Howard pleaded for anyone with information about the suspected hit and run to contact police.

“We don’t want Joshua’s passing to be wasted,” he said.

“We want drivers to know the impact of speeding or whatever happened.

“Think of others. Stop. Ring triple zero and take responsibility for [your] own actions.”

#73. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#70. California investigates Nvidia over whether chip exports ended up in China

State attorney says scandal ‘raises bright red flags’ and aims to find out if company misled investors

California has served an investigative subpoena on Nvidia after a scandal that prompted the departure of co-founder and longtime chief executive Jensen Huang.

Rob Bonta, the US state’s attorney general, issued the subpoena as part of an investigation into whether exports of the California-based company’s advanced semiconductor chips ended up in China, contravening US restrictions.

“The conduct of Nvidia and its former chief executive raises bright red flags, including about the ways in which the company may have misled investors,” said Bonta.

“Nvidia is in a messy, transnational situation but the underlying principle of our corporate securities law is clear: companies cannot mislead their investors.”

The subpoena, issued on Thursday, followed Huang’s resignation a week ago after it emerged he had multiple affairs, including one with Alice Huang, a suspected Chinese spy.

Alice Huang, who is not related, is associated with Singapore-based Megaspeed, which has bought nearly $5bn (£3.8bn) of Nvidia chips and hardware in recent years, and is being investigated for possibly diverting advanced restricted chips to China.

Megaspeed has said it is a “legitimate and lawful” business, and that Huang left the company late last year.

Bonta said the investigation would also look at whether the former Nvidia chief’s actions resulted in confidential information being passed to China.

“My office is investigating whether Nvidia or Jensen Huang’s actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information,” he said. “And if Nvidia misled investors and the public about this risk, in violation of the law.”

Nvidia’s shares are down by a fifth since Huang admitted the extramarital affair, hitting a one-year low at the close of trading on Thursday, and wiping almost $1tn off its market value.

Earlier this week, Harvey Jones, Nvidia’s chair, said the company had no intention of reinstating Huang after the former CEO said he would be “honoured” to return. Elon Musk and a host of tech bosses had campaigned for Nvidia to bring him back.

Huang is due to testify before several congressional committees over the potential breach of national security on 1 December.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Shares in rival chipmaker AMD closed down almost 8% on Thursday after the publication of reports connected to short seller Hunterbrook warning about its links to China and the “circular financing” of customers.

The company, which has been targeted amid the Nvidia scandal, is looking at a further fall of more than 5% in pre-market trading.

Hunterbrook Media published allegations on the use of AMD’s software by a Chinese server maker blacklisted by the US. Sister publication, The Bear Cave newsletter, alleged that AMD’s use of datacentre lease guarantees and backstops for unsold cloud computing capacity could leave it exposed to a pullback in AI spending.

AMD said the reports’ claims “misrepresent AMD’s business practices and relationships”.

“AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations,” the company said. “We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity.”

AMD’s share price has more than doubled so far this year, giving the company a market capitalisation of more than $800m.

#68. In a year of AI solving maths problems, an Anthropic worker says he may have cracked the biggest

Anthropic just went public with a major IPO this week

An Anthropic employee and mathematician claims to have used the company's artificial intelligence (AI) chatbot to prove the famed Riemann hypothesis.

Levent AlpÃ¶ge said, external on Friday, a day after Anthropic made its US stock market debut, that over the course of about six weeks, he used the Claude chatbot to create a detailed proof of the maths problem.

If found to be correct, the proof would be among the most significant in history. AlpÃ¶ge described it as "one dawn across science of something far greater".

The claim comes as AI firms like OpenAI have in recent months announced scores of solutions to major higher maths problems that have eluded scholars for decades.

As for the Riemann hypothesis proof put forward on Friday, it was found using a non-public version of Anthropic's Claude.

It is a nearly 170-year-old maths problem that deals with prime numbers and is widely considered to be the single most famous problem in maths, in large part due to how complex it is.

Named after the mathematician Bernhard Riemann, the hypothesis puts forward that prime numbers have a determined order along the number line.

Prime numbers are the bedrock of higher maths, leaving breakthroughs related to them to have broad impact.

AlpÃ¶ge said that he messaged Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei on Sunday, asking him directly about how he could announce the discovery.

"The precise timing is horrible," AlpÃ¶ge said, referring to Anthropic's public stock listing. "If I could have, I'd have finished this all much earlier."

"I am clearly unilaterally responsible for feeling I just could not live if I waited even longer. There is an obvious cynical view, to do with the timing, but it is false," he added.

The complete textual proof that AlpÃ¶ge put forward in Lean, a computer language used for confirming maths proofs, is over 250,000 pages long.

Terence Tao, a prominent mathematician, said on Friday, external that verification of the proof is still some way off.

"I imagine the digestion process will take some time, though in this case at least we have a human author to communicate with to assist with this task," Tao said.

No such human was obviously a part of major maths proofs made earlier this year using AI.

OpenAI in September claimed that employees using a non-public version of its AI tools were able to solve part of a notoriously difficult problem in just 88 hours. The problem was part of the Navier-Stokes equations, which concern how fluids move.

More than 10,000 AI agents, or computer programmed bots that operate somewhat autonomously, were put to the task as an AI training exercise.

The proof that OpenAI released on Navier-Stokes has yet to be confirmed as accurate, and it set off criticism within academic and higher maths communities and organisations about the use of AI to solve maths problems.

Anthropic has also claimed to have made other progress and solutions in maths, external and physics, external.

A group of maths scholars said, external AI companies boasting of major solutions to show how sophisticated their commercial products had become was "detrimental to the science of mathematics".

"We are witnessing a general threat to intellectual work, with misalignment between the outcome of the use of AI and its initial purpose," the scholars said in a group letter.

A month later, OpenAI released, external what it claimed were solutions, proofs, or major progress on 372 more outstanding higher maths problems.

It will take months, if not longer, for those claims to be reviewed by experts to prove their validity.

#65. Nasdaq erases all 2026 gains as AI sell-off dampens Anthropic’s second day of trading

The Nasdaq lost trillions in value, with the AI startup’s IPO failing to buoy it as investors question huge sums being poured into AI buildout

Who are Anthropic’s seven co-founders, now worth $180bn?

As Anthropic’s stock dropped during its second day of trading, the wider stock market dipped with it. On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite erased all its gains for 2026 and closed down 5% from its value on 1 January. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 closed in correction territory.

Anthropic’s record-breaking IPO failed to stem a global sell-off in AI-linked companies, and the tech-heavy Nasdaq erased some $4tn in market value this week, according to the Wall Street Journal.

Shares in the frontier AI company behind the Claude chatbot, which floated in the US on Thursday, fell by 9% on Friday, ceding most of the gains from the biggest initial public offering in history. They dropped to $88.30, towards the $83 level at which Anthropic priced its stock market debut.

Anthropic’s first two days as a listed company have been volatile. Its shares jumped by 17% on Thursday, after briefly surging by almost 40% when trading in the firm began.

US technology stocks followed Anthropic down on Friday, with the memory chipmaker SanDisk falling 14% and rival Micron down 12%. Nvidia fell 14%, eliminating hundreds of billions of dollars in market cap.

Neil Wilson, a UK investor strategist at Saxo, said there was “just relentless selling in tech”. He warned: “Anthropic shares tumbled to take an $85 handle as the post-IPO euphoria gives way to the reality of a steep sell-off across the tech and AI space that shows no sign of easing just yet.”

Global markets slumped on Friday too; Asian stock markets recorded their worst week since 2020, as shares in big chip companies fell. In Taiwan, stocks dropped 6.7% to hit their lowest point since mid-April, with the chip maker TSMC down about 6%.

The scale of the decline in Taiwan prompted the chair of its stock exchange to pledge to ensure the stable operation ⁠of capital markets, and reassure investors they should remain confident in the fundamentals of Taiwanese companies.

In Japan, the Nikkei index fell 5%, while South Korea’s tech-heavy Kospi index lost 5.5%. Shares in the tech investor SoftBank had their worst day in more than two years, down 14.5% in Tokyo, amid worries around its aggressive bets on AI.

European stock markets posted their biggest weekly loss since March 2025, with the pan-European Stoxx 600 dropping 1.9% on Friday. London’s blue-chip stock index hit a five-month low.

The sell-off followed losses on Wall Street on Thursday, where the main indices all fell by more than 2% on a day dominated by Anthropic’s IPO.

Chris Beauchamp, the chief market analyst at the investing and trading platform IG, said Anthropic could be “draining market liquidity”, as investors sell shares in other companies to raise funds to pay for stock in the newly floated AI firm.

“The slide from yesterday’s highs sends a message that there might be more at work here than investors realise, and while brokers are scrambling to buy more, that liquidity has to come from somewhere, and the other tech names may be the first ones to feel the pressure,” Beauchamp said.

Anthropic’s flotation has failed to ease worries around the AI trade, as investors increasingly question whether companies can deliver the returns necessary to justify the billions of dollars being poured into the AI buildout.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior market analyst at Capital.com, said: “The IPO demonstrated that demand for AI exposure remains strong, but it has not been enough to overcome concerns about elevated valuations, profitability and the sustainability of investment spending.

#63. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.
