#61. Tech stock sell-off drags markets down as Anthropic shares slide

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic's shares have dropped back towards their initial price after a soaring first day on the stock market, as a wider tech sell-off dragged markets down.

The sell-off comes as concerns remain over the speed and size of artificial intelligence investment, but also as some large companies including Nvidia and SpaceX faced specific issues that worried investors.

Those pressures combined to weigh on US markets through Friday, with all key US indexes closing sharply down on the day, furthering earlier falls over the week.

Both the S&P 500 and the Dow Jones Industrial Average were pushed into correction territory, a point where they are trading at 10% or more down from a recent high.

The S&P 500 closed down 3.7%, the Dow Jones Industrial Average was down 1.9% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index plunged 5.3%. However, all three indexes were up on where they were a year ago.

The speed at which AI investment has been growing - with more than $1 trillion spent in the last year alone by a small handful of major tech companies - has led to ongoing concerns of a financial bubble. Questions are being raised over whether those investments will pay off, and when.

Investors were also spooked after the Democrats' resounding win in the US midterms in early November, which sparked fears of a reduction in AI development.

Despite a positive first day of trading, Anthropic's shares fell on Friday, dropping at one point by more than 12% to about $85, close to its initial public offering (IPO) price of $83. It closed down 8.5%, reversing half of the gains made on Thursday.

The artificial intelligence (AI) company's introduction to the Nasdaq stock exchange on Thursday was a crucial test for the industry and was largely seen as a success, with trading starting at $111.50 before easing to end the day up 17% at $97.17.

Analysts said volatility was to be expected in the first days or even weeks of Anthropic's trading, as the company was the first frontier AI firm to go public so had no direct peers.

Some have also noted the similarities with Elon Musk's SpaceX, which at the time of its debut in June was the largest IPO on record. Shares in the rocket and AI company experienced large swings in its first few weeks of trading.

Ed Moya, a strategist at market intelligence firm AlphaSense, said Anthropic's blockbuster IPO did not appear to have restored confidence in the broader AI market.

While AI demand was still very strong, Moya told the BBC that investors were trying to answer the question of how much were they willing to pay for that growth.

"As we've gone through the past past few weeks, it seems that there's been more risks that have surfaced," he said.

"Right now you're starting to see Wall Street is trying to shift from rewarding growth at any price, to demanding stronger cash flow and a clearer path to profitability."

Shares in some of the largest US tech companies also fell on Friday as the market sell-off continued.

Nvidia shares had plummeted by 13.7% by the close of trade, following Reuters reports - unconfirmed by the BBC - the company was being investigated by the US Securities and Exchange Commission over its accounting practices.

Musk's firms Tesla and SpaceX both experienced falls of more than 7.6%. SpaceX shares were hit after it announced a bond sale to finance what the company said was the largest purchase of Nvidia chips on record.

Meta closed down more than 5% and Amazon was down more than 4%, while Apple and Alphabet experienced smaller falls.

Europe's main stock markets - which contain fewer tech stocks - also took a hit.

Germany's Dax index closed down 2.6%, France's CAC 40 was down 1.9% and the UK's FTSE 100 ended trading 1% lower.

Earlier on Friday, tech-heavy Asian markets saw steep falls.

South Korea's Kospi and Japan's Nikkei 225 indexes closed down about 5%, with shares in memory chip makers Sk Hynix, Kioxia and Tokyo Electron, and other tech firms such as Samsung Electronics, seeing losses.

Taiwan's stock market dropped about 6% on Friday, taking the weekly fall to more than 17% and what was reported to be its biggest weekly fall since 1990.

#59. Trump threatens ‘tremendous penalties’ for US tech firms that build datacenters abroad

President gives wide-ranging White House press conference and says he has ‘fantastic relationships’ with Putin and Xi

Donald Trump threatened on Friday to impose “tremendous penalties” on American tech companies that build datacenters outside the US, though he gave no details and told reporters he had not yet informed his own officials about the plan.

In a discursive press conference from the White House briefing room, Trump also said he would “look into” the report that Iran had been planning to possibly detonate a “dirty bomb” in Washington DC, and backed JD Vance over comments the vice-president made blaming Republican midterm losses on the Iran war.

Trump also expounded on his friendship with the Russian president Vladimir Putin, whom he met alongside China’s Xi Jinping at the Apec summit on Thursday, adding he had “fantastic relationships” with both autocratic leaders.

At the White House, Trump told reporters he wanted datacenters built by US companies to stay on US soil.

“We’re not allowing datacenters to go into other countries, because China will take all of them, and we’re not allowing that to happen. And we’re going to put tremendous penalties on companies that try to do it,” he said.

He said Americans should welcome datacenters, and urged communities to extract concessions from developers, repeating a point he made before the midterms.

“You ought to take advantage of the datacenters. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but don’t shut them off,” he said.

With many Americans turning against the datacenters being built near their homes, the US tech industry is making a show of conciliation by promising to take fewer government handouts, to be more transparent, and to give more money to towns and cities willing to allow server warehouses to be built.

The Trump administration on Monday issued new rules to restrict remote access to advanced US computing chips and servers, in a move it said was designed to limit China’s ability to use US chips to train its artificial intelligence models – though a House committee said on Wednesday the US had signed off on sales of nearly 900,000 AI chips to China in six months.

China’s ability to obtain advanced AI chips made by the US firm Nvidia, in which Trump is an investor, has been spotlighted in recent weeks after its CEO, Jensen Huang, was fired over his affair with a woman alleged to be spying for China.

In the same press conference, Trump was asked about reports Iran was planning to potentially detonate an explosive laced with nuclear material – a so-called “dirty bomb” – in Washington DC. The president called it a “rumour” and said: “If they do, we’re going to fight.”

He added: “We know a lot about what’s happening in Iran. We know everything they say. We know who they are, where they are. We’ll find out about it.”

Responding to a question about reports of a rift with Vance, Trump said the vice-president’s remark blaming the Republican collapse on the Iran war was “a very accurate statement”.

“I didn’t say I was unhappy with it,” the president said. “I would have added one other thing if it were asked … would you do it again? And absolutely, the answer is yes, because we cannot allow Iran to have a nuclear weapon.”

Trump was asked whether Vladimir Putin could really be considered a friend to America since Russia is a close ally of Iran.

“I can’t speak to America,” he said, adding: “I consider [that] I have fantastic relationships with Putin. That’s a very good thing. I have fantastic relationships with President Xi. That’s a good thing, and our country is safe because of that.”

He praised Putin as he repeated his grievances over the Department of Justice’s investigation into the Russian attempts to influence US voters ahead of the 2016 presidential election.

“I’ve been dealing with Putin … [since] the fake Russia, Russia, Russia hoax,” he said.

Trump also spoke about two prominent Democrats. He said Gina Hinojosa, who won the race for Texas governor over Trump’s Republican ally Greg Abbott, called him. “She was really nice,” Trump said. “It was a very productive call. And she wants to have a strong border, and I think we’ll be able to work something out.”

And he said Hakeem Jeffries – who Trump previously called a “thug” but is now in line to be the next speaker of the House, and the first Black speaker – is “a smart guy. He’s a streetwise guy.”

#57. French police officers sentenced for beating of music producer Michel Zecler

The three officers were seen on video beating up music producer Michel Zecler in his studio

Three French police officers have been convicted of beating up a black music producer six years ago in Paris in what the court upheld as an aggravated circumstance of racism.

The three were also found guilty of signing a falsified police report stating that the producer - Michel Zecler, 47 - had violently resisted their attempt to arrest him.

Their lies were exposed because the whole scene at Zecler's recording studio was caught on CCTV, revealing the producer had suffered some 30 punches, kicks and baton strikes while offering little resistance at all.

Aurelien Lapersonne, 29, Philippe Tondeux, 50, and Pierre Plantier, 37, all received five year sentences, with different amounts suspended.

Lapersonne had three years of his sentence suspended and Tondeux three and a half years suspended. Plantier had four years suspended, and will serve his one year with an ankle monitor.

All were told they could never serve in the police again.

The case became a cause cÃ©lÃ¨bre for critics of the police, who say it exposed systematic racism among officers. With police again facing criticism for alleged use of excessive force in stopping recent protests, the verdict was hotly awaited by Zecler's supporters.

Much depended in the two-week trial on Zecler's own testimony that the officers called him a racial slur during the beating.

This was denied by the three policemen, who in court admitted the beating and the faked report but denied there was a racial motivation. But the court preferred the evidence of the producer, because - unlike the police - everything else he said was verifiably true.

Because the racist element was accepted by the court, the sentences were toughened.

The case was heard by a panel of five judges.

The incident took place during the Covid lockdown in 2020. Zecler was accused by the officers for not wearing a mask when doing so was obligatory as he approached his studio on foot.

They later claimed his behaviour aroused their suspicions, and they thought he might be carrying drugs. No evidence of this emerged.

After Zecler entered the lobby, the police followed - without a warrant - and tried to bring him outside. Zecler refused to go out, and the beating ensued. Zecler cried for help and some people appeared from the basement and pushed the police outside.

The officers then smashed a glass pane and sent a tear-gas grenade into the building, forcing those inside to come out - after which Zecler was again hit. He was then arrested.

During the trial Zecler said had it not been for the CCTV video, he would have been in court not as a victim but as a potential criminal.

In their last words before the court, all three police officers apologised for what they had done. Tondeux said he was sorry "for the image of the police that I have given".

#55. China commits to low-carbon electrification and help for others to improve

Exclusive: Countries at Cop31 are being urged to commit to deriving 35% of their energy from electricity by 2035

China is pushing for rapid electrification of its economy using low-carbon energy and will help other countries do the same, a senior member of the Chinese delegation at the Cop31 UN climate summit has said.

Wang Yi, a professor of energy and environment policy at the Chinese Academy of Sciences and a senior adviser to China’s government, said: “End-use electrification is a very clear direction. We can follow this direction while making the source of electricity clean. That’s the way to go forward.”

China is the biggest emitter of greenhouse gases by far, producing more than a quarter of the world’s total, so its push for electrification is significant.

At Cop31, countries are being asked to sign up to a target of deriving 35% of final energy consumption from electricity by 2035. Australia, the president of the negotiations, and the host nation Turkey have pushed the target and on Friday night Australia and China signed a joint statement endorsing the “35:35” goal.

But in the second draft text of the probable outcome from Cop31, released on Thursday, the electrification pledge was downgraded to one of a suite of voluntary options countries can take up to reduce their greenhouse gas emissions.

In an interview with the Guardian at the talks, Wang said: “China will do our best to realise clean end-use electrification, and also help other countries to improve. China has significantly reduced the cost of clean technology and products and we would like to share our experience with other countries.”

Experts have said electrification is the next vital step in the shift to a low-carbon economy, as the only way to diminish the close to 80% of fossil fuels still used for primary energy, mostly for transport, heating and industry.

While civil society organisations want any commitment made on electrification to specify that it must be met from clean sources, a pledge that leaves the choice to countries is likely to have more widespread support, as many – including China and India – are resistant to anything they feel smacks of “top down” interference in national plans.

But campaigners argue that allowing individual countries to decide risks leaving the door open to more gas-fired power plants. Some compromise language is also possible that would urge countries to decarbonise their power.

Last year, electricity made up nearly 30% of China’s energy use and it is on track to make that 35% by 2030. Electricity is several times more efficient than fossil fuels, meaning that it takes far less energy to run electric vehicles, heat pumps and industrial processes than their fossil fuel counterparts.

Wang said China was happy with the 2035 target, but that it would be harder to meet if the electricity was required to come entirely from low-carbon sources. “For this target, no problem. But if you think about ensuring clean electrification, that may be a little bit difficult, even for China. For other countries, it’s even more difficult,” he said.

China still has a large number of coal-fired power stations and is planning some new ones, though fewer than before. Wang said this would be used for back-up during the transition to clean energy.

One of the key issues at Cop31 is the “transition away from fossil fuels”, which was one of the resolutions passed at the Cop28 summit in Dubai in 2023, but which countries have failed to flesh out since. The phrase was included in the first draft of the “cover decision” outcome from Cop31, presented on Monday, but excised from the second draftissued on Thursday, to the dismay of many countries and campaigners.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

Wang said: “We agree with paragraph 28 of the UAE consensus [the transition resolution]. But maybe different countries have different roadmaps. It’s very difficult to have only one unified roadmap for every country. So maybe we will help other countries to have their own country-based solutions, to realise their own national way of establishing a new renewable-based power system.”

China is also concerned about trade barriers to its clean technology exports, including the tariffs that the US under Donald Trump has placed on Chinese goods, and the “carbon border adjustment mechanism” (CBAM) imposed by the EU. The CBAM penalises imports from countries with looser regulations on carbon than the EU, which forces companies in high-carbon sectors to pay for their carbon emissions in order to level the playing field.

Some smaller developing countries have criticised the EU for its CBAM, warning that it will hamper their manufacturing industries. The EU said it had processes in place to ensure smaller developing countries were not hurt.

Wang called for more work on trade issues at Cops. “They must come together to find a unified approach,” he said. “China and other developing countries, including the global south, disagree [with the EU] on this. But that’s why we should continue to strengthen our dialogue.”

Jiangwen Guo, a senior research fellow at the Chatham House thinktank, said other countries could learn from China. “China has very consistent top-down policies [and] sets a clear direction with high consistency, then follows with learning by doing,” she said. “In the great energy transition, there is no one ahead of China. China is creating the model for the globe.”

Negotiators at Cop31, which was scheduled to end at 6pm on Friday, were sent home late on Friday after the talks stalled, with a new draft text of a “cover decision” marking the main outcome of the negotiations not thought likely to emerge until some time on Saturday morning. The talks are likely to continue into Saturday and potentially beyond.

#53. Deal with EU not dependent on tuition fees agreement, Miliband says

Watch: Miliband spoke to the BBC following the summit in Brussels

A deal agreed between the UK and the European Union on Friday will come into force regardless of whether the two sides can negotiate a separate tuition fees agreement, Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband has told the BBC.

The measures agreed in Brussels include scrapping most checks on British food exports in return for the UK following some EU regulations from 2028, and a new youth mobility scheme.

However, a proposal from the bloc to lower the level of tuition fees paid by EU students in the UK has not been agreed.

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said she was "confident" a solution would be found, with Prime Minister Andy Burnham adding "me too" during a press conference announcing the deal.

Meanwhile, asked in his interview with the BBC's Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg programme whether rejoining the EU would be looked at, Miliband said "all the options will be on the table".

It came after the prime minister said that he would discuss possible ideas for even closer ties with the bloc in the coming months.

What is the EU-UK youth experience scheme and when will it start?

Burnham gets his first taste of EU dealmaking and seems hungry for more

Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

UK and EU leaders have hailed the closer cooperation agreement, with Burnham calling the deal a "win-win" that could lower the cost of living and reduce red tape.

The prime minister said the Treasury believes the deal could boost the UK economy by up to Â£17bn a year.

EU sources had earlier suggested that the bloc might hold up applying the wider deal until it was agreed that EU students who wanted to study in the UK would pay the same fees as British students.

Miliband said it was "absolutely clear" the agreements on food exports, energy and youth mobility would apply, even though there has not been a deal on tuition fees.

"President von der Leyen... was very clear about this at the press conference we just had. She said there are those three agreements and then there's a separate piece of paper and a separate issue, which is looking at the issue of tuition," he said.

Asked again whether the agreements would definitely become law, the foreign secretary said: "Yes, absolutely."

"The dates are set out in 2028 when these things are coming in, we're introducing legislation in Parliament, so definitely."

Miliband said there would be "no cost" to the UK taxpayer for anything agreed at Friday's summit, adding that there would be no new contributions to the EU budget.

He said previously announced agreements on EU schemes like Erasmus+ were a "separate decision" and the summit "was not about cost to the taxpayer".

Speaking on Friday, Burnham argued that polling suggests the UK public "want closer ties" with the EU and the deal "delivers that".

Miliband told the BBC that "of course all the options will be on the table" when pressed on whether rejoining the bloc would be considered, adding that "we will look at the benefits and the costs of all the different options".

He said that while he accepted the result of the 2016 Brexit referendum, "the world has changed massively".

"Europe is changing too, so we've got to look at what that means. It's not about this parliament, we're respecting the red lines in this parliament," he added.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said the deal was "not fair", arguing that UK farmers would have to follow rules set in Brussels and young people from the EU would "compete" with young Britons struggling to get jobs at home.

She also claimed Burnham had "plucked" the Â£17bn figure out of thin air, and said the only modelling she had seen of how the agreement could boost the economy put the figure at "0.0%".

Reform UK leader Nigel Farage said Friday marked a "betrayal of the British people" and a good day for the EU to "try and humiliate" the UK.

Farage, who was a leading figure in the 2016 campaign to get Britain out of the EU, wrote on social media: "Like every prime minister before him, Burnham is a total fraud."

Liberal Democrat leader Sir Ed Davey said there needed to be more than "tweaks" to the UK's relationship to the EU.

"A youth mobility scheme is a big step forward, but it's nowhere near enough. We need a whole new deal that actually works for Britain, and fast."

Speaking to BBC Radio 4's Today programme, the University of Manchester's vice chancellor Professor Duncan Ivison said closer UK-EU relations were a "good thing" but "the issue of tuition fees is something we do have to figure out because there is a cost".

He warned that EU students being allowed to pay domestic university fees would "take Â£500m, we estimate, out of the higher education sector at a time where there's a lot of pressure on the sector".

He added: "If EU students come and are paying domestic fees, it's not clear we'd be able to increase the number of places for EU students anyway."

Environment Secretary Dame Angela Eagle said the Treasury and Department for Education would work with universities to find a solution during negotiations.

She told BBC Breakfast: "I don't think anybody wants to do deals that negatively impact anything. We want win-win deals."

#50. South Korea education minister resigns after AI cheating scandal engulfs university exams

Choi Kyo-jin said he ‘failed to prepare adequately’ for entrance exams, which this year had phones banned and checks for cameras hidden in clothing

South Korea’s education minister resigned on Sunday after an AI-assisted cheating scandal that erupted in the lead-up to the country’s key university entrance exams.

The country has been gripped by fears of AI-assisted exam cheating since last Sunday, when the top scorer in a national tax accountant exam confessed to using a miniature camera and smartphone to achieve a near-perfect score, just four days before the crucial university entrance exam known as the suneung.

The confession sent officials scrambling to roll out a slew of preventive measures for the entrance exams last week, including blocking public wifi in testing areas.

But the education minister, Choi Kyo-jin, struggled to reassure the public, and teachers’ unions criticised the government response as belated.

The annual eight-hour suneung determines which university a candidate can attend and, in turn, their future prospects.

“The exam concluded without any major incidents … However, as the minister in charge, I do not believe I am immune from public criticism that we failed to prepare adequately in advance,” Choi wrote on X.

“Now that the exam is over, I take full responsibility and hereby offer my resignation,” he added.

President Lee Jae Myung accepted his resignation, the presidential office said.

Asked during a parliamentary hearing on Monday whether he was confident that authorities could catch would-be cheaters on exam day, the 72-year-old Choi replied: “Due to my age, I am not confident I could.” His response quickly drew public backlash.

Choi did not appear at press conferences in the days leading up to Thursday’s exam, where a vice minister instead announced the preventive measures.

South Korea goes to great lengths to ensure the smooth running of the suneung. Aircraft take-offs and landings are rescheduled during the 25-minute English listening paper to minimise noise, while artillery fire and tank movements are suspended. The stock market opens an hour late and cars are stopped 200 metres from every centre.

In the aftermath of the cheating scandal earlier this month, the government announced that smart devices would have to be turned off and handed in for the suneung, while bags left in corridors would be checked for any devices that were still on. Exam officials would also inspect all candidates’ glasses and watches, as well as their collars, sleeves and buttons for concealed cameras.

The government also sent emergency alerts to every phone in the country, which sounded at full volume and could not be silenced, in the hope of catching out anyone who has not handed in their device.

Officials said 111 cases of misconduct involving electronic devices were identified during Thursday’s exam, nearly half of which were detected through the emergency alerts.

#47. Lock of former US President George Washington's hair sells for $4,000

George Washington died in 1799 after serving for two terms as America's first president

A framed lock of hair from America's first president, George Washington, has been sold at auction for $4,000 (Â£3,058).

The hair once belonged to John Gadsby, who entertained Washington in 1799 just before the latter's death the same year, and was later obtained by a collector called John K Lattimer from a descendent of Gadsby's, according to Richmond Auctions, which sold the item.

Saturday's sale is not the most expensive for Washington hair at auction. Three strands thought to have been removed from him close to or after his death stored in a locket sold for nearly $40,000 in 2021.

Gadsby, a resident of what in 1799 was the town of Alexandria in Virginia, entertained Washington for dinner shortly before his death later that year, according to a testament given in 1928 by Gadsby's granddaughter, Julia Ten Eyck McBlair, and which was posted on the Richmond Auctions website.

McBlair stated that her grandfather came into possession of Washington's hair "in view of the friendship and association with his last days" and had it framed and hung "under General Washington's portrait in our home in Washington City, there I have seen it all my life".

Gadsby hosted Washington's two birthday celebrations in 1798 and 1799, Richmond Auctions states.

Washington led American forces to victory against the British in the War of Independence and helped draft the US Constitution before becoming the country's first president in 1788. He stepped down in 1797 after serving two terms.

Several locks of Washington's hair have been preserved, including at least 50 examples that are in a collection at his former residence, Mount Vernon, which is now a museum.

"Public displays of hair from friends or loved ones peaked in the 19th century, but had long been popular among affluent families like the Washingtons," a blog on the Mount Vernon website states.

As well as lockets of his hair, other items associated with Washington have also done well at auction.

In July, a signed letter by Washington from 1776, in which he asks for soldiers for the Continental Army to fight the British, sold for $109,800.

#46. Florida pastor found guilty over 2021 assassination of Haitian president

Christian Emmanuel Sanon faces life in prison after conviction on charges arising from killing of Jovenel Moïse

A Florida pastor with dual Haitian-American citizenship was convicted on Wednesday of US charges arising from the 2021 assassination of the Haitian president Jovenel Moïse.

Christian Emmanuel Sanon was found guilty on charges including conspiring to kill Moïse after a nearly eight-week trial in federal court in Miami. He had faced a total of eight charges including conspiracy to kidnap or kill a person outside the United States.

He faces up to life in prison at sentencing.

Federal prosecutors said south Florida was a hub for planning and financing the plot to oust Moïse and install a president of the conspirators’ choosing. The government said Sanon was the conspirators’ initial choice for Moïse’s replacement. Prosecutors also said Sanon helped raise money for the conspiracy and arranged travel to Haiti and lodging for himself and others in that country.

Sanon acknowledged in trial testimony that he was in communication at one point with people trying to replace Moïse, but pointed out that the group had moved on to another candidate long before the assassination. He denied any knowledge of plans to kill Moïse.

Sanon was born in Haiti and moved to the US as a teenager. He testified that he worked with churches in Haitian immigrant communities in Florida and the midwest.

Haiti has not had a president since authorities said that a squad of mostly retired Colombian soldiers killed Moïse and wounded his wife in a night-time raid at their home on 7 July 2021. The poorest country in the Americas, Haiti has a history of dictatorship, political upheaval and foreign intervention that has long hindered democratic rule.

The election of a new president is scheduled for next year.

Prosecutors said the scheme to kill Moïse included paying about two dozen foreign mercenaries with money obtained from US government programs that were intended to help businesses during the Covid pandemic, according to court records. US prosecutors have charged more than 40 people with crimes related to the assassination and a dozen have been convicted, including five who are serving life sentences.

The plot to oust Moïse began taking final shape in April 2021, when Sanon met in south Florida with a former Haitian senator and several US businesspeople, according to an indictment. The group agreed that Sanon, once in power, would award contracts to two Florida-based companies – Counter Terrorist Unit Federal Academy and Counter Terrorist Unit Security, collectively known as CTU – for infrastructure projects, security forces and military equipment, according to investigators.

CTU initially hired about 18 Colombian nationals with military training to provide security for Sanon, according to prosecutors. Conspirators also spent months obtaining weapons and body armor and attempting to build ties with Haitian gangs, officials said.

By June 2021, the conspirators realized Sanon had neither the constitutional qualifications nor sufficient popular support to become president. They shifted their support to Wendelle Coq Thélot, a former Haitian supreme court judge who died in January 2025 while a fugitive.

Sanon was previously scheduled to be tried with four other men who were convicted earlier this year, but his case was separated from theirs and delayed for health reasons.

#41. Man convicted of starting Palisades fire in Los Angeles

Homes along Los Angeles' Sunset Boulevard could be seen engulfed in flames during the fires of January 2025

A jury in Los Angeles has found a 30-year-old man guilty of starting one of the most destructive wildfires in California's history.

Jonathan Rinderknecht was convicted of arson after starting the fires that swept through Pacific Palisades and parts of Malibu in January last year, killing 12 people and destroying more than 6,800 buildings in the US state.

The conviction was handed down nearly five months after deadlocked jurors led to the judge declaring a mistrial.

Rinderknecht, who had pleaded not guilty, was convicted of two federal charges. He is due to be sentenced on 17 March when he faces between five and 20 years in prison on each charge.

Jonathan Rinderknecht's arrest in October last year

Rinderknecht was found guilty of lighting a fire on 1 January last year which burned undetected deep in root systems before flaring back up six days later.

One of the charges was for starting the original brush fire, while the other centred on the damage caused when it reignited into a catastrophic blaze.

"We were determined to hold him accountable, and today's guilty verdict delivers a measure of justice for the families who lost loved ones and the thousands whose lives were upended," said Bill Essayli, who leads the US Attorney's Office for the Central District of California.

Rinderknecht is a Los Angeles resident and former ride-share delivery driver.

Defence attorney Steve Haney had argued at trial that prosecutors lacked sufficient physical evidence or records to prove their case.

Lawyers for the defendant said they were "disappointed" by the verdict but respected the jury's decision.

Los Angeles Mayor Karen Bass, who was widely criticised for her handling of the fires, said in a statement that "no verdict can bring back the lives lost or erase the pain families have endured".

"So many are still struggling to rebuild," she said. "For those still displaced, the devastation remains a daily reality."

Prosecutors had argued that Rinderknecht was driven by revenge, anger and loneliness because he had nowhere to go for the holiday.

They cited geolocation data from his iPhone, security camera footage that showed his car's location and witness testimony from passengers.

They alleged that Rinderknecht had a "deeply entrenched belief that the wealthy were destroying the world" and in turn purposely started the blaze in the affluent neighbourhood.

The fire was one of several that ignited back-to-back across the LA region in January, which in total left 31 people dead and destroyed 16,000 structures.

#40. Race to be next UN secretary general exposes divisions among world powers

Debate about dominance of permanent five members could reignite if major powers block Costa Rican frontrunner

The race to become the next UN secretary general could reach a decisive moment on Friday when a UN security council vote is likely to reveal whether the Costa Rican frontrunner for the post would be blocked by the big powers. If so, the setback will reignite a debate about the dominance of the permanent five (P5) members at the United Nations.

Rebeca Grynspan, an economist and UN insider who is from a family of Polish-Jewish wartime refugees to Costa Rica, has been in the lead in all but one of the eight straw polls of the 15 UN security council members since the first secret ballot was held on 30 July. Each member goes down the list of candidates, casting an “encourage”, “discourage” or “no opinion” vote for each candidate.

Despite countries not being required to reveal how they voted, it is thought Grynspan’s candidature has been resisted by Russia and the US, two of the five permanent security council members that can ultimately wield a veto against a candidate. Knowledge among the security council delegations of how countries are voting is down to private intelligence, leaks and a process of elimination.

The intensive lobbying over the job has spread across the globe, but Friday is especially important since the poll is slated to differentiate the voting of the P5 from the 10 elected members (E10) of the security council. To be recommended as secretary general to the UN general assembly, a candidate needs nine votes and no vetoes. In Friday’s colour-coded secret ballot, the P5 will cast red ballots and the E10 white.

One diplomat said: “The electoral system means it is important how many votes you win, but equally how many of certain votes you don’t lose.”

The protracted 140-day deadlock confirms the polarisation that has hampered the incumbent secretary general, António Guterres, but also belies claims the role is now an irrelevance.

Friday’s secret ballot may also reveal whether the United Arab Emirates’ campaign for its diplomat, Lana Nusseibeh, is about to blow up in its face due to the hostility to the UAE’s brand of active, commercially driven diplomacy, not just in Sudan, but also Somalia, Western Sahara, Libya and Yemen.

A late entrant, in the two straw polls in which she has contested so far, Nusseibeh secured only seven “encourage” votes, six “discourage” and two “no opinions”. This is despite prestigious big-power backing and her acknowledged personal diplomatic skills.

Nusseibeh has US and Russian backing. The French foreign ministry may have misgivings about the UAE, but Emmanuel Macron, the French president, travelled to the UAE in the midst of French riots to see the UAE president, Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan, and secure 100,000 metric tons of diesel a month.

In the UK, the foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, has kept his counsel, but he shares a Polish-Jewish refugee heritage with Grynspan and cannot be an admirer of the UAE’s human rights record or its evasion of EU sanctions on Russia. The prime minister, Andy Burnham, has UAE connections from his time as mayor of Manchester, and Nusseibeh is a fellow alumnus of the University of Cambridge. At the UN she worked closely with the UK in an informal group called “Friends of the Future of the UN”.

Yet 68 British MPs, including the Labour chair of the international development select committee, Sarah Champion, last week signed a letter calling on Burnham to veto Nusseibeh, describing her as “a tainted and compromised figure”.

Some of the opposition to her from elected security council members is not wholly political. Latin American countries believe an informal rotation system means it is their turn to lead the UN. As a result Panama and Colombia, currently on the security council, will oppose her. China has also been supportive of this rotation principle.

But others deeply object to what they consider to be the UAE’s dollar diplomacy and preparedness to use its extraordinary wealth to exacerbate and exploit pre-existing divisions in a country to gain a commercial foothold. Somalia, for instance, has a seat on the Security Council, and its president, Hassan Sheikh Mohamud, this week went on TV to accuse the UAE of buying ports, establishing military bases and supporting armed forces outside Somalia’s national security framework without federal approval.

It is also possible that Finland and Latvia, the two elected members with the greatest stake in Ukraine, are unimpressed that the UAE gave Vladimir Putin red carpet treatment when he visited Abu Dhabi in December 2023, nine months after the international criminal court had issued an arrest warrant for child trafficking. Greece, less engaged in Ukraine, may be more flexible.

Since the last poll, Nusseibeh has been to Bahrain, Britain and Pakistan, a clue as to where extra votes may be harvested. She also had a “thoughtful exchange” with Zeng Jixin, the Chinese ambassador to the UAE.

The two hurdles are whether someone so deep inside the UAE inner circle would have the courage in office to show true independence, and whether her active denials of UAE involvement in arming the RSF in Sudan leave her irretrievably tainted. From a Palestinian intellectual family, she insists she would make peace in Sudan her number one priority.

In the last ballot on 5 November, Grynspan won 12 votes in favour and three negative. But on 16 October – the only colour-coded ballot so far – which distinguishes between permanent and elected members – she received two discourage votes from permanent members, the equivalent of a veto. Nusseibeh had not joined the race at that point.

In an administration as personalised as the US, it has been hard for Grynspan to gain influence, with many sensing that Mike Waltz, the US ambassador to the UN, is the key influencer.

Grynspan has been reluctant to make the fact she is Jewish a feature of her candidacy, but the election of a Jewish person to run the UN might be seen as something the US could embrace as transformative. Her age – she is 70 – could be considered a drawback among those calling for a younger generation to renew the institution.

Although there are other Latin American candidates, Grynspan is seen as a more viable candidate than her rival, the Argentine Rafael Grossi, the head of the UN weapons inspectorate. Russia has opposed Grossi, objecting to his interventions over the Zaporizhzhia nuclear plant in Ukraine.

One way or the other, the logjam has to be broken soon.

#36. Denmark accuses Russia of plotting to assassinate US-Ukrainian rapper

Denmark has accused Russian intelligence services of plotting to assassinate a Ukrainian-American near Copenhagen, saying they had "planned" and "initiated" the attempt.

Denmark's security and intelligence agency (PET) said it had stopped individuals who were intending to kill the person, named by Danish media as rapper Kyivstoner, at a concert in September. Two people were charged with attempted murder and assisting a foreign intelligence service, and pleaded not guilty.

Denmark's foreign minister said Russia's defence attachÃ© will now be expelled.

Russia's embassy in Denmark called the allegations "unsubstantiated" and said Copenhagen failed to provide evidence of Moscow's involvement.

In a statement, the embassy claimed a "Danish model" had emerged, which it described as a "Russian trace" allegedly being discovered and "exploited for a propaganda spectacle". It accused Denmark of "provocations" intending to "frighten the Danish population in order to justify... a prolonged confrontation with Russia".

Russian state media cite the foreign ministry in Moscow as saying it will respond to the expulsion of its official "accordingly".

In a statement on Saturday, European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said: "I strongly condemn the assassination attempt against a US-Ukrainian citizen in Denmark, orchestrated by Russian intelligence services.

"This was an attack on EU soil. And it is unacceptable," she said, adding that "Russia's hybrid threats are becoming less and less hybrid, and more and more real."

The rapper's real name is Albert Vasiliev.

PET said the individuals who allegedly tried to assassinate him were associated with a banned Danish gang named Loyal to Familia. The group is "known for extensive, serious and violent criminal activity", according to the agency.

PET, which worked with police on the case, said three individuals were stopped while travelling by car near the music venue where Kyivstoner was set to perform on 25 September.

Firearms and knives were found in the vehicle, PET said.

Since then, two people linked to the case have been charged with attempted murder and assisting a foreign intelligence service.

Both individuals pleaded not guilty to the charges, local media reported.

In July, Russia's FSB security service accused Vasiliev and the Ukrainian authorities of organising a "terrorist attack" on military facilities and personnel in Russia. The rapper denied this allegation on social media.

In a statement on Friday, Danish Foreign Minister Lars LÃ¸kke Rasmussen said the alleged assassination attempt was Russian "intimidation" and an attempt to make Denmark "waver" in its support for Ukraine.

Russia's defence attachÃ© must leave the country within seven days, Rasmussen said.

He also said Denmark wants the EU to restrict the ability of Russians to travel throughout Europe, saying even though Copenhagen was already very reluctant to issue visas, Russians could still get a Schengen visa in another country and travel to Denmark.

Copenhagen wants these visas to apply only in the country that has issued them, he added.

#35. ‘Weak and dizzy’: the secretive ICE force-feeding tactics that ended a Cuban father’s hunger strike

Exclusive: Luis Alejandro Cora Pérez says in messages to Guardian from inside Texas facility pain of force-feeding caused him to break strike

The text message that came from inside the Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) detention center was stark: “I have spent five days eating regular food. If I don’t eat it, they put the tube in me to feed me.”

A Cuban asylum seeker, Luis Alejandro Cora Pérez, 32, said in messages to the Guardian this month that he was still weak and dizzy after three months without eating, but that he had broken his hunger strike because being force-fed was just too painful.

The Trump administration responded to requests for comment by denying it had force-fed Cora Pérez. But the detainee sent more details – and his is far from an isolated case.

Cora Pérez was one of two men on hunger striker at a Texas facility for whom ICE sought and received a federal judge’s permission to force-feed in recent weeks, a Guardian investigation found.

Cora Pérez and the other man were locked up inside the Port Isabel service processing center, an ICE detention facility in south Texas, just a few miles from the US-Mexico border, on the coast near where Elon Musk’s SpaceX launches rockets. Cora Pérez began a hunger strike in August to protest against his prolonged detention by federal immigration officials while his case plays out. At first he only drank water, then after three weeks, he says he began to drink one Ensure nutritional shake per day.

On 30 October, court records show ICE received permission to forcibly feed and hydrate him, and to restrain him while doing so.

The next day he says six guards at the center held him down, while medical staffers inserted a feeding tube through his nose and down his throat, and pumped nutritional liquid into his stomach – a process he called “traumatizing, both physically and psychologically”.

On 3 November, ICE then received permission from a federal judge to force-feed another hunger striker at Port Isabel, from Uzbekistan, federal court records show. The DHS says the Uzbek man has not been subjected to the practice, however.

On Wednesday night, after weeks of communications, Cora Pérez was suddenly transferred to another detention center in Texas. His relative says neither of them were alerted to any plans to transfer him, or a reason for it.

The DHS did not specify why Cora Pérez was transferred, adding that ICE makes custody determinations on a “case-by-case” basis based on “various factors”.

The revelations come as ICE’s detention practices continue to come under fire amid allegations of mistreatment, and as an increasing number of sporadic hunger strikes have been breaking out in ICE facilities across the country.

The two men’s cases in Texas, revealed here for the first time, are some of the most recent instances of government lawyers seeking a federal judge’s permission, as required by ICE policy, and receiving the go-ahead to force-feed detained hunger strikers. The Guardian’s reporting is based on court records, interviews, dozens of messages exchanged and an interview conducted with Cora Pérez via a facility phone. It further reveals how the Trump administration is increasingly obtaining such court orders to force-feed detained immigrants, a secretive practice widely condemned as torture by human rights organizations and medical ethics groups.

Detainees driven to hunger strike are typically using it as a last resort to protest against poor conditions and the Trump administration’s preference for arrest and prolonged detention while their civil immigration cases proceed through the system.

Cora Pérez said detention center staff inserted the feeding tube and left it in his body, a not uncommon practice, according to experts, and introduced nutrition – first a “clear liquid”, followed by a “milky liquid”, he recalled – through the tube twice more that day, before he cracked and said he broke his hunger strike, citing what he called “horrific torture”. He began voluntarily drinking nutritional shakes the following day, he said.

“ICE does not hold back on anything, and they do anything they want, like we have seen, even killing their own US citizens,” he wrote to the Guardian, referring to the fatal shootings of Renee Good by an ICE officer and Alex Pretti by a border patrol officer in January of this year during Trump’s pivotal immigration crackdown on Minneapolis.

Detained hunger strikers subjected to involuntary medical procedures, including force-feeding, have rarely spoken out about their cases or revealed their identities, much less while still detained. Cora Pérez’s messages offer a rare glimpse at conditions inside a detention center’s restricted medical unit where hunger strikers and suicidal detainees are held.

“They just took [the Uzbek man] in an ambulance after [him] fainting in front of the medical lobby where they take our vitals, our weight and our temperature,” Cora Pérez said in an alarmed message last Monday morning.

The Uzbek man was returned the following day to the facility, Cora Pérez said.

“I see him every day because I see him in the room in front of me, but they don’t let us talk to each other,” he added.

The Guardian independently confirmed the Uzbek’s man identity but is not publicly naming him, because efforts to contact him have been unsuccessful. The man arrived at the Mexico-US border in April 2022 and was released into the country while navigating his immigration case. He was arrested by border patrol officials in April of this year and placed in detention. The man does not appear to have any criminal record and the DHS did not point to any criminal history.

In a statement, DHS said that after they received the court’s permission to force-feed the Uzbek man, he “has voluntarily taken in fluids and there has been NO force-feeding or forced hydration”.

Security operations at the sprawling Port Isabel facility in Los Fresnos, Texas, are run by a private prison company, Akima Global Services, while healthcare is primarily handled by government workers.

“ICE respects detainees’ rights to exercise self-expression and medical autonomy by refusing nourishment and medical care,” the DHS said in a statement. “ICE Health Services Corps may provide involuntary medical treatment only to prevent imminent life-threatening harm or death. Medical staff may provide treatment only to medically stabilize the patient, bringing the detainee outside the imminent harm, incapacitation, or death.”

The US attorney’s office in the southern district of Texas, which represented ICE in its force-feeding request, did not respond to multiple requests for comment. Virginia-headquartered Akima Global Services, and its parent company Nana Regional Corporation, based in Alaska, did not respond to a request for comment.

Force-feeding cases are shrouded in secrecy. After a detainee launches a hunger strike, ICE often sequesters them in medical isolation cells for monitoring, according to ICE’s detention standards. The DHS said that ICE continues to offer hunger strikers three meals per day.

After a certain period of not eating, which varies per case, ICE will request a federal judge’s permission to force-feed the hunger striker. Many court records, and sometimes entire court dockets, are sealed and hidden from public view. In Cora Pérez and the Uzbek man’s cases, like in others, they did not have any legal representation to challenge the force-feeding requests.

The Guardian found at least five instances in which ICE requested permission to force-feed hunger strikers in Port Isabel since January 2025. The federal judge who ruled on Cora Perez and the Uzbek man, Rolando Olvera, has similarly ordered in at least three other cases, records show. Olvera’s judicial assistant said the court had no comment for this article.

ICE is known to have requested permission to force-feed at least 21 detainees across the US since January 2025, the Guardian found, but the number is probably higher since an unknown number of records and dockets remain sealed, unsearchable and not feasible to locate. Most of the cases coinciding with the second Trump administration and tracked by the Guardian are in Texas and Florida, with others in Georgia, Arizona and Washington state. Under the Biden administration, ICE force-feeding requests were also made in Louisiana and California.

Amy Zeidan, an emergency medicine physician based in Atlanta and an expert on healthcare in immigration detention, said force-feeding hunger strikers can amount to “torture”, and lead to significant psychological distress. She also said it was a violation of medical ethics.

“Essentially it’s like inserting a straw into your nose,” Zeidan said. “You have a gag reflex, so most times people will gag, and that’s really uncomfortable. And people will even vomit repeatedly if it continues to irritate their gag reflex.”

Zeidan added that the forceful insertion of a feeding tube can lead to the swelling of the nostrils and the throat, and even tear them, leading to bleeding.

“It is like torture – somebody is doing a painful procedure to you that you don’t consent to,” Zeidan added. “Autonomy is a fundamental principle of medical ethics. We can’t do something to patients if they don’t want to, even if we think it’s good for them.”

There are numerous cells and rooms inside the Port Isabel medical unit, including isolation cells and shared rooms for ill detainees, according to Cora Pérez. In recent weeks, there were five people in the Port Isabel medical isolation cells used for hunger striking or suicidal detainees, he added. He described the cells as “practically freezing”, constantly surveilled with a security camera, and with just a bed, toilet and sink. As well as himself, he says he observed the Uzbek man, a Middle Eastern man, a “tall” eastern European man and a Black man of unknown nationality in the isolation cells. Unit detainees are escorted by facility staff to showers and sometimes receive yard and television access, he said.

Cora Pérez worries for the other detainees. The Black man, whose identity or nationality he does not know, seems to have “serious mental health problems”, Cora Pérez observed.

“He harms himself and spends the night yelling and hitting the door, and does not let anyone sleep,” he said.

The Guardian has independently confirmed the eastern European and Middle Eastern men’s identities, but is not publicly naming them because efforts to contact them have been unsuccessful. The DHS did not comment on the other men inside the medical isolation unit, saying they could not comment without the Guardian providing their identities.

Cora Pérez had left his son and parents in Cuba and requested asylum at the Mexico-US border in 2023, according to a relative who spoke to the Guardian on condition of anonymity due to fear of retaliation by immigration authorities. Cora Pérez went to live with the relative in Florida, where he worked in maintenance at a hotel and as a food delivery worker, he said.

In March 2025, Cora Pérez was arrested by local police and later convicted of two misdemeanors, including “breach of peace” and “obstructing an officer without violence”, according to local records. He was sentenced to two months in jail, his relative said. But ICE placed a hold on him and as he was being released in June 2025, immigration officials took him into custody – a common system – and soon transferred him to the Adelanto detention center, a privately-run facility in California.

Days in Adelanto turned to weeks, then months, then over a year, according to a federal court order denying one of his petitions requesting his release. His multiple requests to immigration and ordinary federal courts for release from detention, while he fights his asylum case, have been unsuccessful.

“All his claims will be heard by an immigration judge, and he will receive due process. This administration will not ignore the rule of law,” DHS said. “Criminal illegal aliens are not welcome in the US.”

In August, he stopped eating to protest against his protracted detention.

“I have spent two birthdays behind ICE’s bars,” he wrote. “The reasons I did the hunger strike in Adelanto are for my political asylum, for the time I have spent detained, [and] because I have a 14-year-old son who is starving to death in Cuba because he does not have my help.

“Also due to the mistreatment by the officials,” he added.

Cora Pérez did not tell his family about the hunger strike, so as not to further worry them. It was not until he was transferred to Port Isabel in late September that he confessed to his relative he had been on a prolonged hunger strike.

“He doesn’t share details so that I won’t get upset or worry,” the relative said in a phone interview.

Cora Pérez’s mother, who is in Cuba, “does not know anything, because she is sick, and with his detention she has gotten worse”, the relative added, between tears. “I don’t want her nor his grandmother to know anything about it,” the relative added.

On 30 October, the federal prosecutors requested permission for ICE to forcibly feed and hydrate Cora Pérez. Hours later, a federal judge approved the procedures without hearing from Cora Pérez. Cora Pérez told the Guardian in various instances he felt it was unfair he was not given the opportunity to present his case to the judge before the judge approved his force-feeding.

“It’s very disturbing because the judge is basically making a decision on information that is only provided by one side,” said Linus Chan, a professor of clinical law and the director of the detainee rights clinic at the University of Minnesota law school. “That just seems ridiculous.”

Cora Pérez was force-fed the following day, he said in his messages and in a phone call, adding that he did not physically resist because he “had seen a lot of mistreatment of others detained”.

“[ICE does] what they want with us, whatever they want, because a federal judge gave them permission,” he added. “I have suffered on the island [of Cuba] and now at the hands of ICE.”

After asking Cora Pérez about the DHS’s denials that he was force-fed, he repeated that he was. “Brother, ICE says and does whatever they want. They deny, punish, retaliate and do everything at their own will. They have even sent Cubans to Africa,” he said.

The number of people detained by ICE has soared under the Trump administration. And due to policy and legal changes, tens of thousands of people are forced to stay in detention for long periods while they fight their immigration cases.

Chan, from the University of Minnesota, says prolonged detention and delays in their cases lead to logical conclusion. “That’s exactly when people protest,” he said.

“And the only form of protest, for many of these people, is a hunger strike,” he said.

As Trump’s hardline immigration crackdown continues and people are detained as a first resort while their cases play out, Chan said: “Hunger strikes will become more common.”
