#34. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#30. FBI reportedly investigating apparent hack of its criminal database

The FBI is investigating an apparent breach of its criminal information database, US media reports, citing two people briefed on the issue.

The National Crime Information Center (NCIC) database is used by federal and local law enforcement to share information on warrants and background checks.

It would not be the first time hackers have targeted the FBI, the domestic intelligence and security service of the US. The agency did not respond to the BBC's request for comment.

When asked about the breach in a Fox News interview on Saturday, President Donald Trump said he had "just heard" about the hack. "I will say that it's not good. I don't like that," he said.

"But maybe we'll learn things that we would've never learned otherwise," Trump added.

This apparent breach of FBI database comes after a string of tech outages which hit banking, air traffic control and other sectors in the US last week. Officials have said they were not caused by cyber-attacks, but the blackouts have sparked scrutiny from lawmakers.

White House National Cyber Director Sean Cairncross was fired this week, with Trump saying he was not good enough.

Dan Lorhmann, a former analyst for the National Security Agency (NSA), told the BBC News Channel that the NCIC's data included "critical information relating to nationwide criminal activity" and needed to be protected.

He added that while US security agencies including the FBI have "an amazing ability" to keep such information safe" and are "globally respected", the level of cyber-attacks is rising globally.

"There's a lot of different reasons why that's happening but I do have faith in our experts in government," Lorhmann said.

In September, the FBI said it was investigating an apparent breach by a cyber-crime group ShinyHunters, which claimed it had stolen sensitive information on all bureau staff - around 38,000 people.

After that hack, one former FBI agent told the BBC he feared for the safety of current employees, whose personal information could soon become available online to criminals and hostile nation-state hackers.

In a separate development, NBC reported an apparent cyberattack against the US Department of Agriculture (USDA).

Employees were informed on Thursday that its farm payment system had been targeted, NBC said.

The USDA has not publicly commented on the issue.

#28. Haitians excited but uneasy as El Salvador troops fight gangs

Foreign combatants in Port-au-Prince welcomed by many after years of gang control but some fear offensive could exacerbate volatile situation

A bullet-peppered mural of the Haitian striker Emmanuel Sanon covers the facade of Port-au-Prince’s main football ground. But more than two years have passed since any sports stars graced the pitch.

In 2024, as the surrounding city was almost entirely captured by heavily armed gangs, Sylvio Cator stadium – where football greats including Brazil’s Ronaldo and Roberto Carlos once played – also fell under criminal control, its dilapidated red and blue stands used as an outpost by fighters working for a gang boss called Izo. Haiti’s national team, the Grenadiers, found itself homeless and was forced to play its World Cup qualifying games abroad.

That changed last week, as Haitian police and troops from El Salvador reconquered the stadium. Nayib Bukele, El Salvador’s authoritarian president, called the development a step towards delivering Haiti from a criminal insurrection that has displaced more than 1.5 million people and killed thousands.

“We now have the upper hand on the ground,” claimed Bukele, who has sent more than 2,000 soldiers to Haiti since mid-October.

Outside the Sylvio Cator stadium, named after an acclaimed Haitian athlete who won a silver medal at the 1928 Olympics, there were joyous scenes as citizens celebrated a foreign politician they hope will prove an unlikely saviour.

“There’s a wind of hope blowing through our country and, thanks to God, we will get through this. Long live Bukele!” said a 55-year-old decorator who asked to be named as Bòs Baton.

Stood by Sanon’s battle-scarred fresco, Baton recalled being driven from an area called Poste-Marchand after criminals set his home ablaze during an armed uprising that started in February 2024. “Today, we are happy because there are places we couldn’t go to before that are accessible – and it’s thanks to Bukele’s intervention that we can be here today,” Baton said.

Identical sentiments have been voiced on the streets of El Salvador’s capital, San Salvador, in recent years as a result of Bukele’s controversial anti-gang crusade, which has freed millions from gang rule – albeit at a high price.

Since that crackdown began in early 2022, more than 90,000 Salvadorans have been jailed as part of a mass incarceration campaign that has slashed murder rates but also fuelled fears that El Salvador’s democracy has died.

Bukele’s domestic clampdown has made the millennial strongman a darling for rightwing politicians and voters across Latin America and the Caribbean, who hold his actions up as proof that, with an iron fist and a malleable approach to human rights, rampant criminality can be tamed.

Now, as Bukele seeks to extend his seven-year presidency in an election in February, he is hoping to burnish his gang-busting credentials overseas.

“It’s an operation that projects force, that projects Bukele’s image as the saviour … and positions El Salvador as a key reference in the region for [how] … to deal with gangs and security,” said Tiziano Breda, a Latin America regional expert from the Armed Conflict Location and Event Data project.

The extent of Bukele’s ambitions in Haiti is unclear. The operation’s trigger was the 18 October abduction of four Salvadoran aviation technicians who worked for a UN-backed security force. On Monday, Bukele’s vice-president, Félix Ulloa, announced Salvadoran troops would withdraw as soon as the four men were rescued, “because this is not an occupying force – we are not there to stay”.

But Ulloa also said his government was responding altruistically “to a cry for help from the Haitian people and government”, suggesting its mission could be extended.

Bukele has also hinted his plans go beyond short-lived ground operations to recover his citizens, or their remains. “We will triumph. God will give us victory,” the politician vowed this week.

Many Haitians hope his combatants will stay and have been thrilled by cinematic social media clips posted by Bukele showing helicopter strikes on a gang compound and the mansion of a gangster nicknamed Taliban.

Banners and billboards around Port-au-Prince celebrate Bukele as a 21st-century liberator and feature slogans such as “The bandits won’t get a wink of sleep!” and “We’re tired! Give us Bukele!”

Standing outside the liberated football ground, bricklayer Venel Saindor recalled how criminals torched his home in Carrefour-Feuilles three years ago. “It’s impossible to enter my neighbourhood because the thieves are still there. They shoot at us,” the 56-year-old explained, lifting his shirt to reveal a bullet scar above his right nipple.

“We need Bukele to help us return to our homes,” added Saindor, as a Salvadoran soldier with a belt-fed heavy machine gun kept watch from the stadium’s terraces.

Monique Clesca, a writer and activist, echoed the bricklayer’s enthusiasm. “Haitians are elated … [and] I feel elated also … It is a moment of hope,” she said on Tuesday, as thousands of demonstrators poured on to the streets to champion Bukele, chanting: “Viv Bukele!” and “Bukele is not playing games!”

As a sea of protesters swept towards the prime minister’s offices, people from all walks of life spoke of how armed groups had destroyed their lives and their collective hope that Bukele may provide a solution.

“I used to drive an old bus. The bandits burned it. They burned my house down too,” said Wilné Persona, a 50-year-old driver, adding: “Bukele is the one we are waiting for … because we can no longer bear the situation.”

A food vendor called Claudy Mayas urged “Papa Bukele” to stand his ground. “Bukele, I bless you, your wife and your children,” said Mayas, 51. “The Haitian people are with you, just as you are with us.”

Clesca attributed the palpable excitement to the widespread perception that Bukele’s “decisive” action had exposed the incompetence, inefficiency and indifference of Haitian and foreign leaders. “Basically, what Bukele has done is show that leadership is about resolving a problem for your population,” she said, praising how the stadium and other areas were recaptured in less than a month.

Amid the excitement there is also unease. Some fear Bukele’s offensive could exacerbate an already volatile situation in and around Haiti’s capital, much of which resembles a war zone. This week, there was heavy fighting between Salvadoran troops, police and gangs in Village of God, the shantytown that is Izo’s main base. On Thursday, police said officers had killed at least 34 suspected criminals during an operation near the stadium.

Some suspect El Salvador’s president, a social media whiz with 22 million followers on X and TikTok, could be exaggerating his troops’ role in recent successes with help from a strike team of online propagandists and fans. “Nayib Bukele is gang kryptonite,” one US enthusiast gushed this week, calling his intervention “a miracle”.

Others fret over Bukele’s ability to distinguish between gang members and civilians, given the indiscriminate nature of his mass detention campaign in El Salvador, where thousands of innocent people have been jailed without due process.

Clesca downplayed concerns over the toll Bukele’s crackdown has taken on democracy and the rule of law in El Salvador, which now has the world’s highest per capita incarceration rate. Haitian politics was so contaminated with organised crime, she argued, “it’s almost like the mafia has taken over 10 Downing Street”.

“If you’re talking about [an] assault on democracy, Haiti is … almost a case study that is … even worse, more flagrant, than El Salvador,” Clesca said.

The security situation was so dire that urgent action was needed. “Let’s just be honest … in Haiti, we’re being brutalised,” Clesca said. “Girls are being taken as sexual slaves. They’re being gang raped. So somewhere along the line, we have to say: ‘Haiti helped [South American independence hero] Simón Bolívar [who received shelter and support from Haiti in the early 1800s] … and now, Bukele is helping us’.”

At Tuesday’s rally, protesters spoke of El Salvador’s president in divine terms. “Today, we see Bukele as a saviour, as someone sent by God,” said one man, before adding a word of caution that spoke to countless past interventions that have ended in frustration and failure.

“There is one thing I will say to President Bukele,” he said. “He must not start with us and leave us halfway.”

#23. Alleged Mexican drug cartel boss ‘El Chapo Isidro’ arrested in special forces raid

Fausto Isidro Meza Flores was one of FBI’s most wanted fugitives and evaded capture for more than a decade

The Mexican military captured one of the FBI’s 10 most wanted fugitives during a special forces raid in the Mexican state of Sinaloa on Friday night.

Fausto Isidro Meza Flores, known as “El Chapo Isidro”, was arrested along with two other alleged leaders of his gang after a gun battle that triggered widespread panic in the city of Guasave.

The alleged leader of a major drug-trafficking cartel, the Meza Flores Transnational Criminal Organization (TCO), he had managed to evade arrest for more than a decade, even though there was a reward of up to $5m (£3.8m) on his head.

His FBI “most wanted” poster states his organisation “is allegedly responsible for the possession, distribution and importation of large quantities of heroin, methamphetamine, cocaine, fentanyl and marijuana into the United States, and has predominately controlled drug production, transportation and distribution in several locations throughout Mexico”.

Mexico’s defence ministry also alleged he is the leader of the Beltrán Leyva criminal organisation, which is allied to the Mayos, one of two rival factions in the powerful Sinaloa cartel.

Meza Flores’s alleged second-in-command, Germán N, whose alias is “Serafín”, and José N, an alleged financial operator for the Beltrán Leyva group, were also arrested.

The operation to capture the men was carried out by “elite military forces” with help from US intelligence, according to Mexico’s defence ministry.

After arriving in the “target area … using stealth and surprise”, the ministry said the arresting force came under attack “with gunfire and explosives” but managed to enter a house and make the arrests.

The men were then evacuated to Mexico City on a Mexican air force plane to be handed over to prosecutors.

Videos and photos show civilians fleeing gunfire at a baseball game at Kuroda Park stadium in Guasave, as well as burnt-out cars.

A barrage of rapid fire from what appeared to be Mexican security force helicopters hit the ground, according to CNN, and players from the Venados de Mazatlán and Algodoneros de Guasave teams abandoned the field mid-game.

Four Mexican army infantry soldiers died in the operation, the defence ministry said in a statement posted on X that extended the institution’s condolences to their families.

It added that security forces killed seven attackers and arrested eight alleged criminals in the operation, including three who were transferred to Mexico City and five who were turned over to the attorney general’s office in Culiacán, Sinaloa.

Overnight on Friday the ​military operation triggered a wave of violence in the state of Sinaloa, including armed ‌clashes between Mexican security forces and cartel members, who ‌set up flaming roadblocks in some areas.

By ​Saturday the violence had substantially subsided. Sinaloa state authorities ​said “the situation is under control and being addressed by all levels of government”.

Meza Flores was originally indicted in 2012, and in 2013 the US Treasury placed sanctions on him under the Kingpin Act, freezing his assets.

In 2017, the US Department of State’s Narcotics Rewards Program began offering up to $5m as a reward for information leading directly to his arrest or conviction.

He has been charged with multiple counts of drug-trafficking violations, and use and possession of a firearm in the US, and was wanted in Mexico for organised crime and offences against public health.

#22. Mexican drug lord on FBI's 10 most wanted list arrested in shoot-out

Watch: Suspected drug lord 'El Chapo Isidro' transferred to Mexico's capital

A Mexican drug lord who was on the FBI's 10 most wanted list has been arrested following a shoot-out with Mexican authorities.

Fausto Isidro "Chapo Isidro" Meza Flores and seven others were detained by Mexican special forces in Guasave, Sinaloa on Friday, Mexico's defence secretariat said.

Seven suspects were killed in the operation, as were four members of the Mexican army who were travelling to reinforce security in Guasave, the secretariat added.

Meza Flores was wanted in Mexico on organised crime charges, while he has been accused of "flooding the US with fentanyl, cocaine, heroin and other deadly drugs" by the FBI. The US is seeking his extradition.

Mexico has stepped up efforts to tackle drug-related crime after coming under pressure from US President Donald Trump since he returned to the White House.

Meza Flores, the leader of the Meza Flores Organisation, also known as the Guasave Cartel, is thought to have been active in the drug trafficking trade since the 1990s but managed to evade authorities till now by maintaining a relatively low profile.

Fausto Isidro "Chapo Isidro" Meza Flores was detained by Mexican special forces on Friday

Using US intelligence on his whereabouts, Mexican special forces descended on his location using "stealth and surprise", establishing a perimeter to prevent him from escaping and reinforcements arriving, Mexico's defence secretariat said.

However, they "came under attack from gunfire and explosives" before entering the property and arresting Meza Flores, the secretariat continued.

Videos circulating online appear to show helicopters firing streams of bullets towards the ground in Guasave. Others appear to show people at a baseball stadium fleeing as they hear shooting sounds.

Eight people in total were arrested, including Meza Flores' second-in-command, who is also his cousin, and his brother-in-law, who is the group's financial operator, the secretariat added.

Watch: Moment Mexican reporter takes cover on air as gunshots ring out nearby

Meza Flores, his cousin, and his brother-in-law were flown to Mexico City to face prosecution, the defence secretariat said. The other five remained in the state of Sinaloa.

Authorities also said they had seized multiple weapons during the operation, including a grenade launcher, as well as drugs and cash.

The US Consulate General in the city of Hermosillo said it was "monitoring reports of ongoing security operations, armed clashes, road blockades, and vehicle fires that occurred overnight in Ahome, GuamÃºchil, Guasave, and Navolato" following the arrest of Meza-Flores.â¯

It advised US citizens to remain alert for potential violence in the area and for residents to remain sheltered in place.

When Meza Flores was added to the FBI's most-wanted list in 2025, he was accused of "heinous crimes" including kidnapping, extortion and torture.

Then-acting FBI director Brian Driscoll said Meza Flores and his organisation had also unleashed a "scourge of illicit drugs" into the US via its southern border with Mexico.

He was initially charged with drug trafficking in the US in 2012 before being charged again in 2019.

The 44-year-old is alleged to have conspired to manufacture and distribute large quantities of cocaine, heroin, methamphetamine and marijuana between 2005 and 2019.

A number of vehicles were pictured burning on a highway near Los Mochis, Sinaloa, after Fausto Isidro "Chapo Idisro" Meza Flores was arrested

Meza Flores was originally a high-ranking member of the BeltrÃ¡n Leyva Organization, but a split between rival factions led to a surge of violence and prompted him to establish his own cartel by 2013.

His organisation has also engaged in what US officials described as an "extremely violent turf war" with the Sinaloa Cartel, one of the most powerful drug trafficking gangs, which operates in the same region.

Meza Flores was added to the FBI's most-wanted list after Mexican authorities reportedly linked his cartel to 20 million fentanyl pills they seized in 2024.

Mexican President Claudia Sheinbaum has taken a tougher stance on the country's cartels in a bid to resist US intervention on Mexican soil. Mexico has welcomed closer collaboration but resisted US boots on the ground.

Trump made stemming the flow of drugs into the US across its southern border one of his key election pledges.

#18. European Commission accused of sugarcoating figures to release funds to Egypt

Leaked documents raise questions about claims Egypt met welfare targets to qualify for €2.5bn in loans

The European Commission has been accused of sugarcoating figures to enable it to release funds to Egypt under a landmark pact with the authoritarian government of Abdel Fatah al-Sisi.

The charge relates to a €7.4bn deal promised by Ursula von der Leyen, the European Commission president, to al-Sisi on a high-profile visit to Cairo in 2024. The three-year “strategic and comprehensive partnership” was intended to bolster Egypt’s shaky economy, deter migrants from coming to Europe and shore up a region reeling from the 7 October attacks and Israel’s war on Gaza.

The disclosure raises broader questions about the EU-Egypt relationship, as Egyptian authorities face scrutiny over shrinking press freedom following the jailing and alleged torture of six journalists, the entire newsroom of Matsadaash, a factchecking and investigative platform, and the closure last month of one of the country’s last independent news outlets and conviction of its high-profile editor, Lina Attalah.

Attalah is appealing against that conviction and will be in court this week as she seeks to overturn other charges including “publishing false news”, for which she was sentenced to a year in prison in absentia without her knowledge.

The spotlight is on €5bn in EU “soft” loans to Egypt – lending with easy repayment terms, known as macro-financial assistance, that is meant to be available only to countries that respect human rights and democracy. While the EU conditions on democracy and rights are vague, the loans impose much more specific requirements on Egypt to carry out economic reforms.

Separate data sources – from leaked EU documents and deleted from an Egyptian government website – raise questions about the European Commission’s claims that Egypt met targets linked to social welfare programmes, which enabled the EU to release €2.5bn in assistance.

In confidential documents, the commission told EU lawmakers that Egypt had “broadly fulfilled” and then “fulfilled” two separate targets to increase the numbers of poor people on social welfare programmes between 2024 and 2026. But data points to a net reduction of 85,734 households as the Egyptian government removed people from its welfare programmes rather than expanded coverage.

If Egypt had expanded the programmes in line with the EU’s apparent conditions – and not removed any households – about 245,000 households, equivalent to roughly 909,000 people, would be getting these benefits. (The Guardian used World Bank data to make this rough calculation).

According to the leaked documents, the commission wanted Egypt to “further expand cash transfer programmes”, but despite a net reduction in beneficiaries it gave the green light anyway to two separate “soft” loans totalling €2.5bn.

In response to the Guardian’s findings, one of the European parliament’s leading spokespeople on EU-Egypt relations, the Dutch MEP Tineke Strik, said she was introducing a question to the European Commission demanding clarification.

“The finding by this investigation that the commission is seemingly sugarcoating Egypt’s performance is worrying,” Strik said. “From the beginning, the commission has used macro-financial assistance primarily as a tool to lure Egypt into migration cooperation, while considering formal conditions and its reporting obligations towards parliament as an inconvenient formality.”

Under the plan, Egypt has so far received €3.5bn in soft loans in three instalments, with a final payment of €1.5bn expected before the end of 2027.

The charge of sugarcoating figures relates to two social welfare programmes: the Takaful and Karama cash transfer programmes for poor and vulnerable households.

The Takaful (Solidarity) programme gives poor families cash payments as long as recipients have regular health visits and good attendance at school. The Karama (Dignity) programme gives unconditional cash payments to people living with a disability, older people, orphans and widows.

One of the EU conditions for Egypt to gain the first €1bn loan was to increase the number of Takaful and Karama beneficiary households by 100,000 to 4.7 million.

In a leaked report to lawmakers in the EU council of ministers and the European parliament, the commission described that requirement as “broadly fulfilled”.

The data in that report, however, reveals a net fall of 62,281 households during this period, owing to what the EU officials describe as “a multi-annual recertification process”. Some beneficiaries were added to the list but a larger number were removed because they were deemed ineligible.

In order to achieve a later instalment of €1.5bn, Egypt was required to “further expand” the programmes, adding 60,000 households to the Takaful and Karama beneficiaries list. A second leaked report to EU lawmakers shows that the commission concluded the target was “fulfilled” because 442,118 families were added to the list.

On this occasion, the commission did not report on numbers removed. Figures from Egypt’s ministry of social solidarity, however, show a net reduction of 23,453 beneficiaries to the programmes in the 11 months until June 2026, the period leading up to the release of that payment. The EU announced the release of the €1.5bn payment in July 2026.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

The data has since been deleted from the Egyptian ministry website but the Guardian has seen screenshots and archived webpages.

During a period from 2024 to 26 when the EU required Egypt to add 160,000 households to the rolls of the Karama and Takaful programmes, there was a net decline of 85,734 households.

Timothy Kaldas, the deputy director of the Tahrir Institute for Middle East Policy, said the commission had generally “done a poor job of leveraging the funds of European taxpayers to support genuine stabilisation” on the other side of the Mediterranean. “The strategic interest of Europe is seeing a more economically stable Egypt with a population that is less precarious,” he said.

Sandra Gómez López, a Spanish socialist MEP who recently visited Cairo to assess human rights in Egypt, said the data presented by the Guardian raised important questions. “The commission must explain transparently why it considers these commitments to have been fulfilled and demonstrate that European funding is genuinely contributing to improving social protection for the Egyptian population,” she said.

She stressed that the EU should not withdraw support, “but neither can we allow the country’s strategic importance to lead us to lower our standards when it comes to human rights”.

For critics, the questions about the EU’s willingness to hold Egypt accountable on very precise socioeconomic targets do not bode well for the broader but less defined goals for democracy and human rights.

Strik said: “The fact that Egypt and the commission even seem to ignore the crystal-clear socioeconomic conditions doesn’t increase my expectation that they adhere to the vague precondition on democracy and the rule of law.”

Claudio Francavilla, an associate EU director at Human Rights Watch, said: “Without benchmarks, the EU has turned a human rights conditionality into a blank cheque for Egypt’s authoritarianism.”

In a statement to the Guardian, the European Commission said the policy target on Takaful and Karama referred to new beneficiary families “and not to its net increase”. The spokesperson said there was “no contradiction” between this statement and that in a confidential document that said Egypt was required to “further expand cash transfer programmes”.

Asked about the release of the remaining €1.5bn in promised loans, the commission said it “will be made once all conditions have been met”, referring to 24 economic policy conditions, keeping an IMF programme on track and “taking concrete and credible steps towards respecting effective democratic mechanisms, including a multiparty parliamentary system, and the rule of law, and guaranteeing respect for human rights”.

It added: “Compliance with the conditionality is examined thoroughly” by EU officials and diplomats based on “information provided by the Egyptian authorities and other relevant sources”.

Egypt’s mission to the EU in Brussels did not respond to a request for comment.

#17. Fight over fossil fuels leaves COP31 climate talks in deadlock

Will COP31 end with a new global climate deal?

Negotiations at the COP31 UN climate summit in Turkey are in deadlock and have been suspended for the night, after running more than a day over time, as countries have been unable to agree on a new global climate deal.

The EU and Pacific island nations are amongst a group pushing back against the latest draft agreement which included no reference to reducing the world's demand for fossil fuels.

A second sticking point involves a push from the Africa Group to get commitments on more money for adaptation - but the EU has previously said it would not support this.

Negotiating teams due to fly out on Sunday morning are now moving their flights to Monday in a sign that a deal is not yet in sight.

If countries cannot reach consensus - which is required by the UN's climate talks process - the negotiations could be suspended.

In that case the summit would end with no deal and be reconvened next year. This has only happened once before since countries started holding COP climate meetings in 1995.

Panama's Environment Minister is pushing for language on fossil fuels to be in the final agreement

On Saturday, EU Commissioner for Climate Wopke Hoekstra said if the bloc could not get "what is needed in terms of climate action... then there's no deal."

The UK's chief negotiator, Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh, remained more hopeful and said her team was still working to get an agreement.

"These are difficult negotiations, but discussions remain productive and there is a deal to be done. The UK is pushing hard for an outcome and we will keep working in the coming hours to bring countries together."

Another risk is that negotiating teams have to start journeys home, and the meeting could lose the critical number of nations needed to pass any global agreement.

More climate money for Africa? Europe says use what's already pledged

Ministers from Panama, Solomon Islands, Cote d'Ivoire and the EU are fighting to get a commitment to move away from oil, gas and coal back into the final agreement after fossil fuel producers led by Saudi Arabia pushed for its removal.

"It is not acceptable to have a COP or a cover decision that does not even mention fossil fuels and we simply will not put up with it," said Juan Carlos Navarro, Panama's minister for the environment.

His words were echoed by France's Minister of Ecological Transition, Monique Barbut.

She took aim at several references in the text to carbon capture, use and storage - the technology that takes emissions when they are released and stores them underground - as a licence for other countries to keep burning oil and gas.

"We cannot be announcing to the world that we are now getting to 1.5 increase in the temperature and do business like nothing was happening," Barbut said.

For the first time at a COP the agreement - known officially as the Antalya Accords - could acknowledge that the goal of keeping global temperature rise below 1.5C has been missed.

The world is entering a period called "overshoot".

Beyond this point scientists warn, external that millions more people will be exposed to sea level rise and extreme heat.

But Chris Bowen, Australia's climate change and energy minister who is leading negotiations, pushed back on the need to include language on fossil fuels.

He argued that this summit was about implementation, and should focus on the move towards clean energy sources.

Away from COP, renewable energy is growing at unprecedented rates. For the first time this year a modern renewable source - solar panels - was the biggest contributor to meeting new global energy demand, outstripping oil and gas, external.

The International Energy Agency also warned this week that the longer disruption to fuel supplies through the Strait of Hormuz due to the Iran War continues, the stronger the incentive to switch to electricity powered by renewables.

Although, this week US President Donald Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin agreed at a meeting in Shenzhen to increase oil production in the face of fuel supply disruption.

It is understood that one proposal is to keep fossil fuels off the table in exchange for Saudi Arabia and other petro-states signing up to a pledge for 35% of their energy consumption to come from electricity by 2035.

This does not solve one of the other key issues raised by developing nations - the lack of money.

Earlier versions of the text included an agreement to ramp up money for adaptation to $120bn (Â£91bn) by 2035 - that figure has now been removed.

"We did not come here to beg, we came here to claim what is rightfully ours for our people who are suffering the brunt of anthropogenic activities of developed countries," said Zanetor Agyeman Rawlings, Ghana's Environment Minister and chair of the African Group of negotiators.

Climate activists and environmental groups, who are observers to these talks, have slammed negotiations.

"I can tell you without any doubt that this has been the worst COP ever," said Mohamed Adow of Powershift Africa.

These groups are normally highly critical of the process for not moving fast enough. But it seems that Turkey have been particularly unpopular as co-hosts after their lead negotiator walked out of some crucial negotiations leaving many countries feeling "disrespected", said one observer.

#12. Fears grow that US will put Nobel-winning judge under sanctions after Myanmar genocide ruling

A ruling against Myanmar could set precedent for court’s judgment on Israel’s actions in Gaza

Fears are growing that the US could impose sanctions on judges at the international court of justice, including this year’s Nobel peace prize winner, Navi Pillay, if the court rules on Thursday that the Myanmar government committed genocide against the Rohingya.

The ICJ has never found a state responsible for committing genocide, and a significant factor in previous cases has been how to prove “intent” in a crime defined as acts committed with the “intent to destroy, in whole or in part” a national, ethnical, racial or religious group.

Lawyers representing Rohingya survivors say patterns of behaviour by Myanmar’s military, including killing people as they fled, show clear attempts to destroy the group during assaults on Rakhine state in 2016 and 2017.

It is the role of independent judges to interpret the genocide convention. However, judges at the court have taken advice on how to protect their assets from potential US sanctions, as a genocide ruling could be interpreted by Washington as lowering the “legal threshold” for the crime.

The Foundation for Defense of Democracies, an influential thinktank close to the White House, has predicted a genocide ruling will lead to the US imposing sanctions on the ICJ.

The US and Israel fear the final judgment may clarify how to establish a government’s intent to commit a genocide, and so set a precedent that makes it more likely the court will later rule that Israel committed genocide in Gaza.

Myanmar, which has denied carrying out a genocide, claims its actions targeted a military uprising by insurgents.

The ICJ, sometimes described as the UN’s top court, is responsible for settling interstate disputes, unlike the international criminal court (ICC), which prosecutes individuals accused of war crimes and other international crimes.

The US has already imposed crippling sanctions on judges at the ICC after the court issued arrest warrants against the Israeli prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu. The US has also announced sanctions against the entire ICC as an institution, which it described as a “rogue court”. Washington fears the ICC could go after US troops.

The Gambia, as a litigant, was entitled to appoint an ad hoc judge in the case against Myanmar and chose the South African lawyer Pillay, who in October was awarded the Nobel peace prize. She is required to act as a jurist and not an advocate for the Gambia.

Mike Waltz, the US ambassador to the UN, has sent a warning shot to Pillay and the 15 ICJ judges, saying Washington “will not allow globalist, faux judges to weaponise the law against our troops”.

Waltz asserted last month: “The ICJ ad hoc judge Navi Pillay is tipping the scales on the ICJ’s current proceedings on Myanmar, intending to lower the legal threshold for proving genocide so that weakened and false standard can later be weaponised against American and allied soldiers whenever they raise a finger against terrorists that hide behind civilians.”

In remarks addressed to Pillay, he said: “We see your agenda and we’ll be watching the ICJ’s handling of these proceedings very closely.”

In a speech last Friday, the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, said: “For this global elite, international law … is an instrument to advance their interests under the pretence of universal principles.”

Our US morning briefing breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

He warned the ICC against quoting laws to “men with swords”.

Israel has previously criticised adverse advisory opinions issued by the ICJ and refused to implement 2024 orders requiring Israel to admit more aid into Gaza, and to cooperate with Unrwa, the UN relief and works agency.

In the Myanmar case, a 24-page submission jointly filed by the UK and five other mainly European states, argued that the ICJ, in judging intent to commit genocide, should examine a range of “facts and circumstances” assessed “comprehensively and holistically”.

It also stressed that genocide was not solely defined by killings, and that the treatment of children, gender-based violence and forced displacement were also relevant.

The US until recently had taken a relatively more benign view of The Hague-based ICJ, and under Antony Blinken as US secretary of state determined that Myanmar had committed genocide.

The 15 ICJ judges are elected for nine-year terms by the UN security council and the UN general assembly.

Rubio has given the ICC until April to respond to his criticisms before he imposes sanctions.

On Friday, the ICC formally decided to drop plans to prosecute US soldiers and intelligence agents for potentially unlawful acts in Afghanistan. The shelving of the near-dormant inquiry came after it was agreed evidence would be hard to obtain, but one side-effect may be a greater willingness on the part of the US to abandon its plan to impose sanctions on the ICC.

#11. Russia-bound ship attacked in Gulf of Finland

The Finnish Border Guard is leading the operations involving the Sparta II

A Russian cargo vessel bound for the country's Baltic exclave Kaliningrad has been hit by a drone attack in the eastern Gulf of Finland, Russian and Finnish authorities say.

The ship - the Sparta II - caught on fire after the strike on Saturday morning, before drifting into Finnish waters. Finnish defence forces said Ukraine was likely behind the attack. Kyiv has yet to comment.

"War is spilling over into our immediate vicinity," Finnish Prime Minister Petteri Orpo said. "Ukraine has the right to fight back against the aggressor."

Kaliningrad Governor Alexei Besprozvannykh said two drones had been used, based on preliminary information, and there was "no threat" to the territory's supplies.

The Sparta II was sanctioned by the US in 2022 and Ukraine earlier this year, and is run by a Russian company, according to the Ukrainian and US governments.

The company says on its website that it was established within the structure of Russia's defence ministry in 2011 and since 2015 has been operating within the military construction complex.

Finland's coast guard said it had been alerted to the attack at around 08:50 local time (06:50 GMT) on Saturday and that the cargo vessel had been located approximately 50km west of the island of Suursaari in the Gulf of Finland.

This prompted "temporary aviation and maritime restrictions" around the area, Finnish border officials said.

Shortly after the incident, Finland's defence ministry said a Russian helicopter was suspected of violating the country's airspace, potentially linked to "rescue or tracking operations".

A Russian rescue vessel approached the ship and was told by the crew that they did not need to be evacuated, Kaliningrad's governor wrote on Telegram, adding: "The crew is currently carrying out the necessary work and plans to continue on its way."

The Finnish Border Guard later said the crew had been evacuated to another Russian vessel that was now already en route to Russia. The Russian rescue vessel had remained at the location of the stricken ship.

It added that no environmental damage had been detected and that there was no danger to maritime traffic or people.

Kaliningrad is a Russian exclave that sits between Poland and Lithuania.

Defence Minister Antti HÃ¤kkÃ¤nen said that the Finnish Border Guard were responsible for the investigation into the attack and were co-operating with other relevant authorities.

Nato has also been informed, the defence ministry said.

The Baltic Sea has become a flashpoint in the Russia-Ukraine war, triggered when Russia launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022. Ukraine has recently ramped up its long-range attacks against Russia beyond the front lines.

Earlier this month, a Ukrainian drone strike hit a Russian warship in the Gulf of Finland.

#8. One in four women globally unable to refuse sex within marriage, says UN

Almost half of women in west and central Africa cannot withhold consent, with girls worst affected, UNFPA finds

One in four women around the world have no power of consent if their husband or partner demands sex, according to a UN report.

In west and central Africa, almost half of women (45%) are not able to say no in their own marriage, while a third of women (33%) in east and southern Africa and in Arab states face the same predicament. In Latin America and the Caribbean, one in 10 women are not able to refuse sex, said the report by the UN Population Fund (UNFPA) published on Thursday.

Younger women and girls aged 15 to 19 had even less power to say no, compared with older women aged 45 to 49, according to analysis in the report of eight countries with comparable data (Angola, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Kazakhstan, Malawi, Mali, Nigeria, Tajikistan and Zambia). The findings align with research showing adolescent girls endure very high rates of intimate partner violence.

“These are really sad figures,” said Jean-Paul Murunga, a lawyer with the global human rights organisation Equality Now. “Especially given that the continent of Africa as a whole has adopted the Maputo protocol, which sets [binding legal standards for] equality between women and men. We still have a lot of work to do to ensure women and girls benefit from these commitments.”

The Maputo protocol, a landmark treaty adopted by the African Union in 2003, guarantees women’s rights and obliges states to protect women from violence. It affirms that marriage must be based on full consent and guarantees women’s right to health.

“The underlying principle is unambiguous: sexual intercourse without free and voluntary consent constitutes sexual violence, including within marriage,” said Murunga. “Marriage does not create perpetual or implied consent. A woman retains the right to decide whether, when and how she engages in sexual activity”.

He said that as a first step rape laws across the continent needed to change. According to Murunga’s 2024 report, rape within marriage is not criminalised in some African countries. Seven countries expressly exempt husbands from prosecution for marital rape, including Côte d’Ivoire, Ethiopia and South Sudan. Some countries, such as Tanzania and Eritrea, only criminalise marital rape when the couple do not live together.

In countries where child marriage is permitted and marital rape is not criminalised, children are left unprotected. A legal loophole is created for what, outside marriage, would be classed as rape, and young wives have few options when it comes to protecting themselves from sexual violence and exploitation. In Gabon, for example, when a man has married a child he abducted, he can only be prosecuted after the marriage is annulled.

Underpinning these laws is the mistaken belief that marital rape does not exist as a concept because, by marrying, wives are assumed to have permanently consented to sex with their husbands. In many areas, religion plays a key role in affirming these beliefs.

Alimatu Dimonekene, founder of A Girl At A Time, an organisation supporting adolescent girls in Sierra Leone, said a conversation around consent in marriage had yet to begin in Africa. “We’ve not even started the conversation [around marital rape],” she said. “Women will never call it rape; they make excuses for it or even blame themselves.”

She added: “In west Africa, sex is seen as an obligation in marriage. You must do whatever your husband demands.

“Once he has paid your bride price [a traditional marriage custom in which the bride’s family are paid money, livestock or goods by the groom], he owns you and you cannot reject his demands. Whatever he wants, he gets, including in terms of intimacy, finances and raising the children.”

#7. Zelensky says Crimean Bridge strikes 'fair response' as he meets UK's Streeting

Wes Streeting told Volodymyr Zelensky he was in Kyiv to show that Europe was united in its support for Ukraine

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has justified Kyiv's repeated attacks on a critical bridge linking Russia to occupied Crimea, calling it a "fair response" to 109 separate Russian attacks on bridges in Ukraine.

In comments to the BBC in Kyiv, Zelensky said the intense attacks on Ukraine by Russia that have followed the strikes on the Crimean Bridge were only a "signal that Vladimir Putin does not want to stop. He wants a reason to escalate".

He rejected the claim by the Russian president that the bridge - which links Russia to the peninsula it illegally annexed from Ukraine - was "exclusively civilian" in use.

"There are facts, not just the words of Putin. We can see," he said.

The Ukrainian president pointed to images online of tanks and other Russian military hardware using the route from Russia into occupied Crimea as a supply line and for logistics.

"If Putin used the internet, he could see that."

The Ukrainian president added that Russia had carried out attacks with missiles and drones on bridges in Ukraine.

This week's combined missile-and-drone strikes by Ukrainian forces destroyed the central road section of the Crimean Bridge, collapsing part of it together with one of the two support arches into the sea and rendering the structure unusable for road traffic.

Russian attacks on Ukraine continued on Friday evening and overnight: a seven-year-old boy was killed in missile and drone strikes on Zelensky's hometown of Kryvyi Rih in the central Dnipropetrovsk region. Local officials say he had been in a playground at the time.

A 20-year-old female medic was also killed. A local official added that when rescuers rushed to the scene, the same site was targeted again with jet powered drones. Another 58 people were injured in the city.

Ukraine's state emergency service DSNS said 11 people were also injured in the north-eastern Kharkiv region, and one person in Rivne, in the north-west.

Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.

Footage shows Ukrainian missile approach Crimean Bridge

Zelensky was speaking ahead of a meeting in Kyiv with UK Defence Secretary Wes Streeting, who told the Ukrainian president he was here to show that Europe was united in its support.

"The more Putin tries to divide us, the more resolute we become," Streeting said.

He said that Russian missiles hitting Poland on Friday, during military exercises of the so-called Coalition of the Willing, was an "outrageous provocation".

The timing, Streeting said, seemed "more than a coincidence".

Asked by the BBC about Nato's response, he said the best way was to "do more for Ukraine" to defend itself against Putin who he called a "cold-blooded killer".

Streeting's description of Putin chimed with the comments made earlier by Zelensky, who described the Kremlin leader a "killer". This was done in response to US President Donald Trump referring this week to Putin as his "friend".

Streeting's visit to Kyiv came after European leaders meeting in Berlin on Thursday alongside US envoys said they were ready for negotiations with Moscow on ending the war in Ukraine.

"Europe has a clear and consistent and united negotiating position," UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham said, while German Chancellor Friedrich Merz stressed that Moscow's "maximalist demands are not realistic".

Donald Trump's son-in-law Jared Kushner, one of the two US envoys who were in Berlin, said the US president - who had also dialled into the meeting from Air Force One - wanted to make a deal with Moscow "with Europe by his side".

Russia's Vladimir Putin has so far rejected any European role in the US-led peace talks - but earlier this week he invited European representatives to Moscow as it wanted to clarify "what Europe will bring to these talks".

That meeting in the German capital came hours after Putin had held historic trilateral talks in Shenzhen, China, with Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping.

Zelensky said he had been "sceptical" ahead of the latest round of talks, when US envoys claimed that Russia was prepared to make compromises to end the war.

"I thought [Putin] would find reason to escalate."

Speaking to the BBC, Streeting added: "Putin has absolutely no business playing the victim. He is the aggressor in this war. He provoked an illegal invasion of Ukraine. And all of us, right throughout the world, need to judge Putin not on his words, but on his actions."

In a separate development on Saturday, Finland said it "detected a drone strike on a Russian merchant vessel" in the eastern Gulf of Finland, "in an international maritime area".

The Finnish defence ministry said in a statement that the vessel was damaged, without giving any further details.

It added that "temporary restrictions on aviation and maritime areas in the eastern Gulf of Finland" were now reinstated for safety reasons.

Russia has not publicly commented on the issue.

#6. Race to replant five South African baobabs after export blocked

The trees, intended for an unnamed buyer in Georgia, were reportedly dug up without the necessary permits

Environmentalists in South Africa are racing to replant five centuries-old baobabs after the 20-tonne trees were dug up by an exporter, who tried to ship them thousands of miles to Georgia.

The baobabs arrived at a reserve in Timbavati, in north-eastern South Africa, on Wednesday, where they will be replanted and supported by cables to help them recover. The trees had been stranded at a warehouse in Durban since July, when they were intercepted by authorities who said they had been moved without the necessary permissions.

The department of forestry, fisheries and the environment confirmed the trees had been destined for Georgia but did not name the buyer.

The case echoes an episode in Kenya four years ago, when eight baobabs were uprooted to be exported to Shekvetili Dendrological Park, owned by Bidzina Ivanishvili, an influential Georgian billionaire and the country’s former prime minister. Ivanishvili has filled the park with ancient, giant trees from Georgia, transporting them by boat along the Black Sea.

The export of the Kenyan baobabs was initially blocked after an outcry by environmentalists, who condemned their export as biopiracy, but later allowed to go ahead.

Otherwise, such exports are very rare, say experts. David Newton, programme office director for east and southern Africa for Traffic, an NGO that monitors the trade in wild animals and plants, said: “It is very unusual to see baobabs being used as ornamental plants … mainly because they are exceptionally difficult to dig out of the ground and transport, and then to successfully re-establish.”

Sarah Venter, founder of the Baobab Foundation, who has led efforts to replant the South African trees, said she believes they have a good chance of surviving if they are replanted quickly. The winter months of May to October, when the trees lose their leaves and are dormant, is a good time to move them.

“As soon as spring starts [in November], the trees use their water to flush their new leaves, and that’s when we need to get them into the ground as soon as possible,” she said.

The rescue mission has faced delays because of the challenges associated with moving such large trees, which are transported on special trucks capable of carrying abnormal loads. The five baobabs weigh between 19 and 29 tonnes, have a diameter of up to 4.5 metres (15ft) and a height of up to 12.5 metres.

Baobabs, known as the tree of life, are protected under South African law, which means a permit is required to cut or disturb them.

“A baobab isn’t just part of an ecosystem, it is an ecosystem in itself,” said Wambui Ippolito, a landscape designer, lecturer and horticulturist from Kenya. “These trees are biodiversity anchors.”

Animals ranging from bats and bush babies, to grey-headed parrots and the southern ground hornbill shelter in its cavities, while elephants, monkeys and warthogs feast on its fruit.

The trees have huge cultural importance, added Ippolito. “It has always been a sacred tree in Africa. It doesn’t matter where you go: Senegal, Mali, Ghana – wherever you go, if there is a community around a baobab tree, that tree is sacred,” she said. “Somebody’s great-great-great-great-great-grandparents sat under that tree, and it is part of human history in that area.”

Baobabs have shallow roots, which means they do not need to be planted very deeply, and these regrow fairly quickly, said Venter. Once replanted, the trees will be stabilised with cables and protected by electric fences to prevent damage from elephants.

The trees are thought to be between 200 and 300 years old. “Baobabs easily reach 1,000 years old, so they’ve got a few hundred years ahead of them if they survive,” said Venter.

A criminal case has been opened against the owner of a landscaping company allegedly responsible for the illegal harvesting and transportation of the trees.

Find more age of extinction coverage here, and follow the biodiversity reporters Rebecca Ratcliffe, Phoebe Weston and Patrick Greenfield in the Guardian app for more nature coverage.
